Enriching a predicate and tame expansions of the integers
Gabriel Conant, Christian d’Elbée, Yatir Halevi, Léo Jimenez, Silvain Rideau-Kikuchi

Abstract
. Given a structure ℳ \mathcal{M} and a stably embedded ∅ \emptyset -definable set Q Q , we prove tameness preservation results when enriching the induced structure on Q Q by some further structure 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} . In particular, we show that if T = Th ​ ( ℳ ) T=\textnormal{Th}(\mathcal{M}) and Th ​ ( 𝒬 ) \textnormal{Th}(\mathcal{Q}) are stable (resp., superstable, ω \omega -stable), then so is the theory T ⁡ [ 𝒬 ] T[\mathcal{Q}] of the enrichment of ℳ \mathcal{M} by 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} . Assuming simplicity of T T , elimination of hyperimaginaries and a further condition on Q Q related to the behavior of algebraic closure, we also show that simplicity and NSOP 1 pass from Th ​ ( 𝒬 ) \textnormal{Th}(\mathcal{Q}) to T ⁡ [ 𝒬 ] T[\mathcal{Q}] . We then prove several applications for tame expansions of weakly minimal structures and, in particular, the group of integers. For example, we construct the first known examples of strictly stable expansions of ( ℤ , + ) (\mathbb{Z},+) . More generally, we show that any stable (resp., superstable, simple, NIP, NTP 2 , NSOP 1 ) countable graph can be defined in a stable (resp., superstable, simple, NIP, NTP 2 , NSOP 1 ) expansion of ( ℤ , + ) (\mathbb{Z},+) by some unary predicate A ⊆ ℕ A\subseteq\mathbb{N} .



1.
Introduction

The work presented in this article began when the authors met to discuss two open questions from the literature on stable expansions of the group of integers. Recall that
(\mathbb{Z},+)
is a canonical example of a group whose theory is superstable, but not
\omega
-stable. Over the last several years, there has been an extensive amount of work on classifying (or at least cataloguing) stable expansions of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
. See
[
Con19
,
Con18
,
CL20
,
Haw22
,
LP20
,
PS18
]
. A compelling phenomenon is that every proper stable expansion of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
identified in these previous sources is superstable of
U
-rank
\omega
(recall that
(\mathbb{Z},+)
itself has
U
-rank
1
).
1
1

1

By the
U
-rank of a structure, we mean the
U
-rank of its complete theory.
This raises the following questions, which are asked implicitly in
[
Con19
, Question 7.10]
.

Question 1
.

(a)

Is there a strictly stable expansion of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
?

(b)

Is there a superstable expansion of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
with
U
-rank not equal to
\omega
?

The strategy undertaken to answer this question (which we will do) led us to a much broader line of research and more general theorems. In particular, our results not only add a significant new dimension to the understanding of stable expansions of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
, but also to other forms of tameness in expansions of arbitrary weakly minimal structures, as well as the fully general question of preserving dividing lines when enriching the induced structure on a stably embedded definable set. Thus, before discussing the answer to the above question, we will first introduce what we mean by “enriching structure”, and describe our general results on preserving model-theoretic tameness in this context.

An appropriate starting point is the general framework of “model theory over a predicate”. As described very nicely by Baldwin and Benedikt in the introduction of
[
BB00
]
, this research area can be divided to at least two main subtopics. The first deals with the extent to which one can learn about a structure
(\mathcal{M},P)
from the induced structure on
P
alone. See
[
BB00
]
for some specific references for this line of research. The second subtopic deals with a kind of converse question: To what extent do model-theoretic properties of a structure survive after naming a new unary set? Usually one aims to preserve dividing lines such as stability, NIP, simplicity, etc., and there is a significant amount of existing literature along these lines. For example
[
BB00
]
and
[
CZ01
]
focus on preserving stability when naming a new predicate, while
[
CS13
]
extends this to NIP. Those sources also draw from earlier work of Poizat
[
Poi83
]
on
belle paires
, which has led to similar such notions as lovely pairs
[
BYPV03
]
,
H
-structures
[
BV16
]
, and more.

Our work connects to the second subtopic above, and can be seen as a
general
continuation of this line of thought. Let
\mathcal{M}
be a structure, and suppose one is given an
\emptyset
-definable set
Q
(e.g., a unary predicate). Then most reasonable forms of model-theoretic tameness (e.g., stability), will be inherited by the structure on
Q
induced from
\mathcal{M}
. Suppose now that we enrich the
\mathcal{M}
-induced structure on
Q
to some expanded structure
\mathcal{Q}
, which still maintains some level of tameness. Is the corresponding enrichment of
\mathcal{M}
still tame? As we will see below, provided one further assumes that
Q
is stably embedded in
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{M})
,
2
2

2

Stable embeddedness is necessary; see Example
2.10
.
then many dividing lines yield a positive answer (under additional assumptions in some cases).

As far as we are aware, the only result of this nature in the literature is the preservation of NIP, which appears in
[
JS20
, Proposition 2.5]
and
[
CS15
, Lemma 48]
(see Fact
3.6
below). In
[
JS20
]
, this result is used to prove NIP for various valued fields (in the spirit of Ax-Kochen-Ershov) by enriching the residue field in a way that preserves NIP. The first main goal of this article is to prove analogous preservation results for other model-theoretic dividing lines. With an eye toward Question
1
, we will start with various forms of stability.

Theorem A
.

Let
T
be a complete theory and let
Q
be an
\emptyset
-definable stably embedded set in
T
. Fix an arbitrary structure
\mathcal{Q}
expanding
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}
, and let
T[\mathcal{Q}]
be the corresponding enrichment of
T
. Then
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is stable (resp., superstable,
\omega
-stable) if and only if
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
are stable (resp., superstable,
\omega
-stable).

This result is proved in Section
3.1
(see Theorem
3.3
). The proof stems from the fact that, since
Q
is stably embedded, we can characterize types in
T[\mathcal{Q}]
by means of certain types in
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
(see Proposition
2.8
). The preservation of stability can also be proved in a more combinatorial fashion along the lines of the proof for NIP in
[
JS20
]
(see Remark
3.4
). For superstability, the proof is more intricate and relies on Shelah’s saturation spectrum
[
She90
, Theorem VIII.4.7]
. As a result we obtain in Corollary
3.5
a negative answer to the question of existence of a strongly dependent field which eliminates
\exists^{\infty}
, as opposed to fields of finite dp-rank
[
DG17
, Lemma 2.2]
. The idea behind this example is due to Martin Hils.

In Section
3.2
, we first recount the analogue of Theorem
A
for NIP (due to
[
CS15
,
JS20
]
), and then we prove the analogue for NTP
2
using various key results of Chernikov and Hils
[
CH14
]
(see Propostion
3.7
).

We then turn to the dividing lines of simplicity and NSOP
1
. For these notions of tameness, we will prove conditional preservation results which require some further assumptions. For one, we need to assume the starting theory
T
is simple with elimination of hyperimaginaries. Moreover, we assume that the definable set
Q
satisfies an additional coherence property for algebraic closure, which we refer to as “algebraic embeddedness” (see Definition
4.11
). Although somewhat technical, this condition appears to be rather natural, and holds for several examples from the literature (see Sections
4.2
and
7.2
). In any case, with these extra assumptions we prove the following preservation result for simplicity and NSOP
1
.

Theorem B
.

Let
T
and
\mathcal{Q}
be as in Theorem
A
. Assume further that
T
is simple with elimination of hyperimaginaries, and that
Q
is algebraically embedded in
T
. Then
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is simple (resp.,
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
) if and only if
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
is simple (resp.,
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
).

This theorem is proved in Section
4.5
, and combines Theorem
4.35
and Corollary
4.36
. The proof proceeds by characterizing (Kim-)forking independence over models in
T[\mathcal{Q}]
in terms of forking independence in
T
and (Kim-)forking independence in
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
. Simplicity of
T
and algebraic embeddedness of
Q
are used in several steps of the forking calculus. A crucial step is proving the independence theorem for the relevant independence relation in
T[\mathcal{Q}]
. This is done by first amalgamating types in
\mathcal{Q}
and then amalgamating (strong) types over
\mathcal{Q}
. To carry out this latter amalgamation, we introduce in Section
4.4
a structure
\mathcal{H}
consisting of all definable finite covers of
\mathcal{Q}
. We then show that if
\mathcal{Q}
is NSOP
1
, then so is
\mathcal{H}
, and we then proceed with amalgamating types in
\mathcal{H}
. Whether these extra assumptions are necessary remains an interesting question for future work (see the discussion at the end of Section
4.5
for further details).

In Sections
6
and
7
we turn to applications of the above theorems. Recall that our initial motivation was Question
1
above. Even with Theorem
A
in hand, one does not immediately obtain positive answers to the two parts of Question
1
because of the fact that the induced structure on any infinite definable set in
(\mathbb{Z},+)
is essentially the same as
(\mathbb{Z},+)
. So, for example, finding a strictly stable enrichment of the induced structure on a definable set in
(\mathbb{Z},+)
is roughly equivalent to the first part of Question
1
. Thus we will first need to expand
(\mathbb{Z},+)
by a unary predicate with less complicated induced structure. Here we take a more general perspective and work with “eventually indiscernible sequences” in arbitrary structures (see Definition
6.1
). Given any structure
\mathcal{M}
, if
Q\subseteq M
is enumerated by such a sequence then the induced structure on
Q
is interdefinable with a pure set (see Lemma
6.5
). Moreover, if
\mathcal{M}
is
weakly minimal
(i.e., superstable of
U
-rank
1
) then, using results from
[
CZ01
]
and
[
CL20
]
, we can name
Q
by a predicate, without losing stability, and then apply the above preservation theorems to
T=\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{M},Q)
. Given any arbitrary structure
\mathcal{N}
, we expand
\mathcal{M}
to a structure that
names

\mathcal{N}
, meaning we add a predicate for
Q
and an isomorphic copy of
\mathcal{N}
with universe
Q
. This leads to the following result, which is proved in Section
6.2
(see Theorem
6.10
).

Theorem C
.

Assume
\mathcal{L}
is countable and
\mathcal{M}
is weakly minimal. Let
\mathcal{N}
be a stable (resp., superstable,
\mathrm{NIP}
,
\mathrm{NTP}_{2}
) countable structure. Then there is a stable (resp., superstable,
\mathrm{NIP}
,
\mathrm{NTP}_{2}
) expansion of
\mathcal{M}
naming
\mathcal{N}
.

The dividing lines of simplicity and NSOP
1
are omitted from the previous result because, in the above setup, we do not necessarily know that an eventually indiscernible sequence will enumerate a set satisfying the “algebraic embeddedness” condition necessary for Theorem
B
. However, in the special case that
\mathcal{M}
is
(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
, we will show that such sequences can be found (e.g., the sequence of factorials; see Theorem
7.13
). Thus we obtain the following main result regarding tame expansions of the group of integers (which combines Theorem
7.2
and Corollary
7.15
).

Theorem D
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a stable (resp., superstable,
\mathrm{NIP}
,
\mathrm{NTP}_{2}
, simple,
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
) countable structure. Then there is a stable (resp., superstable,
\mathrm{NIP}
,
\mathrm{NTP}_{2}
, simple,
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
) expansion
\mathcal{Z}
of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
naming
\mathcal{N}
. Moreover, the
U
-rank of
\mathcal{Z}
is at least that of
\mathcal{N}
.

This result immediately gives a positive answer to both parts of Question
1
. We also note that, while NIP expansions of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
are well known (e.g., Presburger arithmetic
(\mathbb{Z},+,<)
), and there has been some work on simple expansions (see Section
1.2
), to our knowledge there are no properly NTP
2
or NSOP
1
expansions of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
appearing in previous literature.

Our main preservation results can also be used to answer an assortment of other related questions about tame expansions of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
and weakly minimal structures in general. In the remainder of this extended introduction, we will briefly summarize several of these applications.

1.1.
Expansions of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
by unary predicates

With the exception of
[
Haw22
]
, essentially all of the previous literature on stable expansions of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
focuses on expansions of the form
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
where
A
is some subset of
\mathbb{Z}
. It is thus natural to ask if a positive answer to Question
1
can be obtained using expansions of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
by unary predicates, rather than by more complicated structures. In Section
7.3
, we prove the following result (a special case of Theorem
7.16
), which shows that any countable graph can be coded into an expansion of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
by some unary predicate, while preserving various kinds of model theoretic complexity.

Theorem E
.

Let
Q=\{n!:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
, and suppose
E
is an arbitrary graph relation on
Q
. Define
A=Q\cup\{a+b:(a,b)\in E\}
.

(a)

(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
is interdefinable with
(\mathbb{Z},+,Q,E)
.

(b)

(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
is stable (resp., superstable, simple,
\mathrm{NIP}
,
\mathrm{NTP}_{2}
,
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
) if and only if
(Q,E)
is stable (resp., superstable, simple,
\mathrm{NIP}
,
\mathrm{NTP}_{2}
,
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
). Moreover, the
U
-rank of
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
is at least that of
(Q,E)
.

The previous result applies more directly to
[
Con19
, Question 7.10]
(which motivated Question
1
above). In particular, this question from
[
Con19
]
focuses on the
(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
-induced structure on a subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
, which we denote
A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
. The question asks whether there is a subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is strictly stable, and also which (countable) ordinals
\alpha
can be realized as the
U
-rank of
A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
for some
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
.
3
3

3

In
[
Con19
]
, the focus on subsets of
\mathbb{N}
, rather than
\mathbb{Z}
, is done for thematic reasons related to the discussion at the start of Section
1.3
.
We will use Theorem
E
to provide a positive answer to the first question about strictly stable induced structure (see Corollary
7.21
(a)
). As for the latter question about possible
U
-ranks in induced structure, we first note for context that in the current literature, all examples of stable structures
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
, with
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, yield induced structure
A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
of
U
-rank
1
. Shortly after
[
Con19
]
appeared, the fourth author pointed out that one can obtain finite
U
-ranks using sumsets. However, before the present work, there was no known example of a set
A\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
with
A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
superstable of infinite
U
-rank. See Corollary
7.21
for details.

1.2.
Simple expansions of
(\mathbb{Z},+)

In
[
KS17
]
, Kaplan and Shelah showed that if
P
is the set of integers whose absolute value is prime then, assuming a number-theoretic hypothesis called
Dickson’s Conjecture
,
(\mathbb{Z},+,P)
is supersimple of SU-rank
1
and unstable. Using a similar strategy, Bhardwaj and Tran
[
BT21
]
showed that if
S
is the set of squarefree integers then
(\mathbb{Z},+,S)
is supersimple of SU-rank
1
and unstable (without any conditional hypotheses). For both of these results, the proofs involve substantial machinery from number theory (e.g, the proof of instability for
(\mathbb{Z},+,P)
uses the work of Green and Tao on arithmetic progressions in primes). Thus it is natural to ask for a more combinatorially straightforward way to obtain a properly simple expansion of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
. In particular, Theorem
E
shows that one can simply add a random graph on top of the factorials.

A common feature in the examples
(\mathbb{Z},+,P)
and
(\mathbb{Z},+,S)
above is that both
P
and
S
are subsets of
\mathbb{Z}
that are neither bounded above nor bounded below. This property is crucial for simplicity in these examples. Indeed, using classical number-theoretic facts, it is not hard to show that if one expands
(\mathbb{Z},+)
by a predicate for the primes, or a predicate for the
positive
squarefree integers, then the resulting structure defines the ordering on
\mathbb{Z}
(e.g., this follows from
[
Con19
, Corollary 8.17]
). These observations led the first author to ask whether one could obtain a properly simple expansion of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
by naming a subset of
\mathbb{N}
(see
[
Con19
, Question 8.19]
). The intuition for a possible negative answer was that sparse subsets of
\mathbb{N}
tend to yield stable expansions of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
, while dense subsets can be used to define the ordering via sumsets. Nevertheless, Theorem
E
provides a positive answer.

1.3.
Expansion by mutually algebraic structure

A recurring theme in the study of stable expansions of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
is that given some
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, if
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
is stable then
A
must be quite sparse. For example,
any
sumset
A+\ldots+A
must have upper Banach density
0
(see
[
Con19
, Theorem 8.8]
or
[
Con21
, Corollary 6.2]
). This drastic level of sparsity led the first author to conjecture (in a preliminary version of
[
Con19
]
) that if
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
and
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
is stable, then
(\mathbb{Z},+,B)
is stable for any
B\subseteq A
. Other evidence was the fact that this property holds for all examples from the sources mentioned above. However, the fourth author soon noted that the conjecture is false (see Proposition
7.20
below).

It was later understood that the hereditary nature of stability in the examples from previous work could be accounted for by finer model-theoretic properties of the induced structure. Then in all of the examples from
[
Con19
,
Con18
,
LP20
,
PS18
]
of sets
A\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
for which
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
is stable, it turns out that
A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is superstable of
U
-rank
1
with trivial forking (e.g., for many examples,
A_{\textnormal{ind}}^{\mathbb{Z}}
ends up being interdefinable with an expansion of
(\mathbb{N},x\mapsto x+1)
by unary predicates). In this case, it follows from work of Laskowski
[
Las13
]
that for any
B\subseteq A
, the expansion
(A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}},B)
is again superstable of
U
-rank
1
(with trivial forking), and thus the same holds for
B^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
. It then follows that
(\mathbb{Z},+,B)
is stable (e.g., using Fact
6.8
below).

The conclusions of the above discussion were previously accounted for in
[
CL20
, Theorem 3.21]
(in a general group-theoretic context). However, the following more nuanced question was left open. Specifically, suppose
A\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
is as in the previous paragraph, and
B\subseteq A
. Then
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
and
(\mathbb{Z},+,B)
are both stable; but what about
(\mathbb{Z},+,A,B)
? Even more, suppose we expand
(\mathbb{Z},+)
by predicates for
all
subsets of
A
. Is this still stable? A related situation is considered by Lambotte and Point
[
LP20
]
, who show that if
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
is enumerated by a “regular” sequence, and
s_{\!A}
denotes the successor function on
A
, then
(\mathbb{Z},+,A,s_{\!A})
is stable.
4
4

4

In some cases,
s_{\!A}
is definable in
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
, but this need not hold for all
A
in
[
LP20
]
.
In Section
6.3
, we use our preservation result for superstability, together with work of Laskowski
[
Las13
]
, to prove a broad generalization. Given any set
A
, we let
A^{\textnormal{ma}}
denote the collection of all
mutually algebraic
relations on
A
(see Definition
6.15
; examples of such relations include subsets of
A
and injective functions from
A
to itself).

Theorem F
.

Suppose
\mathcal{M}
is weakly minimal and has constants for an elementary substructure. Assume
A\subseteq M
is such that the
\mathcal{M}
-induced structure on
A
is weakly minimal with trivial forking. Then
(\mathcal{M},A^{\textnormal{ma}})
is superstable.

The previous result is proved in Corollary
6.19
below.
A corresponding version also holds when
\mathcal{M}
is only stable/superstable, provided that the set
A
is assumed to be definable in
\mathcal{M}
(see Theorem
6.18
). Returning to our discussion of the integers, suppose
A\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
is such that
A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is weakly minimal with trivial forking (recall that this includes all examples from
[
Con19
,
Con18
,
LP20
,
PS18
]
). Then
(\mathbb{Z},+,A^{\textnormal{ma}})
is superstable. By the definition of mutual algebraicity (see Example
6.16
), this expansion includes predicates for
all
subsets of
A
,
all
bounded-to-one functions on
A
(e.g., the successor function
s_{\!A}
),
all
bounded degree graphs on
A
, and more.

Notation and conventions

We use small letters
a,b,c
for tuples and capital letters
A,B,C
for sets. We also employ the standard model-theoretic abuse of notation and write
a\in A
to denote that
a
is a
tuple
of elements from
A
, when the length of the tuple is immaterial or understood from context. When working in a monster model
\mathcal{U}
of some theory
T
, we assume that all tuples and sets are strictly smaller in cardinality than the saturation of
\mathcal{U}
, unless otherwise stated or implied from context. We also abuse notation and write
AB
for
A\cup B
.

Given a structure
\mathcal{M}
with universe
M
, and an arbitrary subset
X\subseteq M
, we denote by
X_{\textnormal{ind}}^{\mathcal{M}}
the relational structure with universe
X
and induced structure from
\emptyset
-definable sets in
\mathcal{M}
.
When
\mathcal{M}
is understood from context we may use
X_{\textnormal{ind}}
in place of
X^{\mathcal{M}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
. Given a parameter set
A\subseteq M
, we let
\mathcal{M}_{A}
denote the canonical expansion of
\mathcal{M}
by constants naming all elements of
A
.

If
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
are structures with the same universe
M
(but possibly different languages), then we say that
\mathcal{M}_{1}
is a
definable reduct
of
\mathcal{M}_{2}
if every subset of
M^{n}
definable in
\mathcal{M}_{1}
(with parameters) is definable in
\mathcal{M}_{2}
. We say that
\mathcal{M}_{1}
and
\mathcal{M}_{2}
are
interdefinable
if they are definable reducts of each other.

We identify an automorphism of a structure
\mathcal{M}
with its canonical extension to
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{eq}}
.
When working with a fixed theory
T
, we use the letters
M,N
for small models of
T
, and thus use the same notation for the universe of the model.

2.
Preliminaries on stably embedded sets

Let
T
be a theory in a (possibly multi-sorted) language
\mathcal{L}
.

Definition 2.2
.

A definable set
Q
is
stably embedded
in
T
if for any model
M
of
T
, any
M
-definable subset
X\subseteq Q(M)^{n}
is
Q(M)
-definable.

Throughout this section, we let
Q
be a fixed
\emptyset
-definable stably embedded set in
T
.
We note the following easy observation.

Remark 2.3
.

Stable embeddedness of
Q
is preserved after naming constants, i.e., for any
M\models T
and
A\subseteq M
,
Q
is stably embedded in
\operatorname{Th}(M_{A})
.

We wish to understand types over
Q
by means of a type with small domain. This will be done in the following results, some of which are folklore but never written down to the best of our knowledge.

We first consider the case that
T
is stable. Note that in this case, the assumption that
Q
is stably embedded is redundant.

Lemma 2.4
.

Assume
T
is stable, and let
M
be a model of
T
. Fix a finite tuple
a\in M
and a set
B\subseteq M
.

(a)

There is a tuple
d\in Q(M)
, with
|d|\leq|T|
, such that for every
N\succeq M
,
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/Bd)\vdash\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/BQ(N))
.

(b)

If
T
is countable and
\omega
-stable then in
(a)
we may find
d\in Q(M)
with
|d|
finite.

Proof.

Part
(a)
. As
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/BQ(M))
is definable, for every formula
\varphi(x,y,z)
there is a formula
\psi_{\varphi}(y,z,b_{\varphi,a},d_{\varphi,a})
, with
b_{\varphi,a}\in B
and
d_{\varphi,a}\in Q(M)
, such that
for any
b\in B
,

M\models\forall z\in Q\,(\varphi(a,b,z)\leftrightarrow\psi_{\varphi}(b,z,b_{\varphi,a},d_{\varphi,a})).

Let
d=(d_{\varphi,a})_{\varphi}
. Suppose
a^{\prime}\in N\succeq M
is such that
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/Bd)=\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a^{\prime}/Bd)
, and assume that
\varphi(a,b,c)
holds for some
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(x,y,z)
and parameters
b\in B
and
c\in Q(N)
. Since the formula
\forall z\in Q\,(\varphi(x,b,z)\leftrightarrow\psi_{\varphi}(b,z,b_{\varphi,a},d_{\varphi,a}))
is in
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/Bd)
, it follows that
\varphi(a^{\prime},b,c)
holds.

Part
(b)
. Assume
T
is
\omega
-stable. Then every complete type
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/BQ(M))
is definable over a finite subset of
BQ(M)
(see
[
Mar02
, Corollary 6.3.6]
). The rest is as in part
(a)
.
∎

If
T
is not stable, we can still find such elements, but we might need to relax the bound on the cardinality. In order to have some canonicity, imaginaries will be required.
As
Q
is
\emptyset
-definable, any
\emptyset
-definable equivalence relation
E
on
Q^{n}
extends to an
\emptyset
-definable equivalence relation
E^{\prime}(x,y)\coloneqq E(x,y)\vee(x\not\in Q^{n}\wedge y\not\in Q^{n})
on all
n
-tuples. Thus the quotient
Q^{n}/E
can be identified with an
\emptyset
-definable set
D_{E}
in the
E^{\prime}
sort of
T^{\mathrm{eq}}
. Given a model
M\models T
, we let
Q^{\mathrm{eq}}(M)
denote the union
\bigcup_{E}D_{E}(M^{\mathrm{eq}})
where the index ranges over all such
E
on
Q^{n}
for all
n
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
M
be a model of
T
.

(a)

For any
B\subseteq M
and finite tuple
a\in M
, there exists
d\in Q(M)
, with
|d|\leq|B|+|T|
, such that for every
N\succeq M
,
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/Bd)\vdash\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/BQ(N))
.

(b)

For any
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(x,z)
(with
z
a
Q
-variable), there exists an
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{eq}}
-formula
\psi_{\varphi}(u,z)
and an
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{eq}}
-definable function
f_{\varphi}(x)
into
Q^{\mathrm{eq}}(M)
such that for any
a\in M
,
\varphi(a,Q(M))=\psi_{\varphi}(f_{\varphi}(a),Q(M))
.

(c)

Fix
B\subseteq M
and a finite tuple
a\in M
. Define the tuple

c=(f_{\varphi}(a,b):b\in B,\,\varphi(x,y,z)\text{ an $\mathcal{L}$-formula})\in Q^{\mathrm{eq}}(M)\cap\mathrm{dcl}^{\mathrm{eq}}(aB).

Then
|c|\leq|B|+|T|
and, for every
N\succeq M
,
\mathrm{tp}^{T^{\mathrm{eq}}}(a/Bc)\vdash\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/BQ(N))
.

Proof.

Recall that
Q
is definable and stably embedded. So by compactness, for any
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(x,z)
, with
z
a
Q
-variable, there exists an
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\psi^{*}_{\varphi}(v,z)
such that for any
M\models T
and
a\in M
, there is some
d_{\varphi,a}\in Q(M)
such that
\varphi(a,Q(M))=\psi^{*}_{\varphi}(d_{\varphi,a},Q(M))
.

Part
(a)
. Fix
B\subseteq M
and
a\in M
. Define the tuple
d=(d_{\varphi,a,b})_{\varphi,b}
from
Q(M)
where
d_{\varphi,a,b}
is as above, and the index ranges over all
\mathcal{L}
-formulas
\varphi(x,y,z)
and tuples
b\in B
. Now fix
N\succeq M
. We show
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/Bd)\vdash\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/BQ(N))
. Without loss of generality, assume
N
is sufficiently saturated. Let
a^{\prime}\in N
be an arbitrary realization of
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/Bd)
. Fix a formula
\varphi(x,b,c)\in\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/BQ(N))
with
b\in B
and
c\in Q(N)
. By elementarity, we have
\varphi(a,b,Q(N))=\psi^{*}_{\varphi}(d_{\varphi,a,b},Q(N))
. Thus
\varphi(a^{\prime},b,Q(N))=\psi^{*}_{\varphi}(d_{\varphi,a,b},Q(N))
since
a^{\prime}\equiv^{T}_{Bd}a
. So
\varphi(a^{\prime},b,c)
holds.

Part
(b)
. Let
c_{\varphi,b,a}
be the canonical parameter for the formula
\psi^{*}_{\varphi}(d_{\varphi,b,a},z)\wedge z\in Q
. Then
c_{\varphi,b,a}\in Q^{\mathrm{eq}}(M)\cap\mathrm{dcl}^{eq}(aB)
. Let
\psi_{\varphi}(u,z)
be the projection of
\psi^{*}_{\varphi}(v,z)
to the sort of
c_{\varphi,b,a}
, and let
f_{\varphi}(x,y)
be the
\emptyset
-definable function (in
T^{\mathrm{eq}}
) mapping
a
to
c_{\varphi,b,a}
.

Part
(c)
. This follows from part
(b)
using an argument similar to part
(a)
, but working in
T^{\mathrm{eq}}
with
\psi_{\varphi}
instead of
\psi^{*}_{\varphi}
.
∎

Remark 2.6
.

The following are immediate consequences of Lemma
2.5
.

(a)

For any
M\models T
and
B\subseteq M
, every
B
-definable subset of
Q(M)^{n}
is definable over
\mathrm{dcl}^{\mathrm{eq}}(B)\cap Q^{\mathrm{eq}}(M)
. In particular, pulling back to
Q
by a
\emptyset
-definable map, the same holds for definable subsets of
Q^{\mathrm{eq}}
.

(b)

On the other hand, if
B
is an elementary substructure of
M
, then no imaginaries are needed, i.e., every
B
-definable subset of
Q(M)^{n}
is
Q(B)
-definable.

Given
M\models T
and
B\subseteq M
, let
Q^{B}_{\textnormal{ind}}(M)
denote the induced structure on
Q(M)
from
B
-definable sets in
M
(in other words,
Q^{B}_{\textnormal{ind}}(M)
is
Q(M)^{M_{B}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
).
We write
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}(M)
for
Q^{\emptyset}_{\textnormal{ind}}(M)
.

We now fix an arbitrary expansion
\mathcal{Q}
of
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}
in some language
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
, which we assume (without loss of generality) to be disjoint from
\mathcal{L}
. Let
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]=\mathcal{L}\cup\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
and let
T[\mathcal{Q}]
be the corresponding enrichment of
T
by
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
on
Q
. In the following results, we assume the existence of saturated models. This can be achieved under some set-theoretic assumptions which do not affect the validity of what we want to prove, see e.g.
[
HK21
]
.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
M,N\models T[\mathcal{Q}]
be saturated models of the same cardinality. Then any
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-isomorphism from
\mathcal{Q}(M)
to
\mathcal{Q}(N)
extends to an
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]
-isomorphism from
M
to
N
. Consequently,
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is complete, the definable set
Q
is stably embedded in
T[\mathcal{Q}]
, and the
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]
-induced structure on
Q
is
\mathcal{Q}
.

Proof.

Pick an
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-isomorphism
f\colon\mathcal{Q}(M)\to\mathcal{Q}(N)
. Since
T
is complete, we may also choose an
\mathcal{L}
-isomorphism
i\colon M\to N
. Let
i_{0}
be the restriction of
i
to
Q(M)
. Then
i_{0}^{\text{-}1}f
is an automorphism of
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}(M)
, and thus by stable embeddedness of
Q
in
T
,
i_{0}^{\text{-}1}f
extends to an
\mathcal{L}
-automorphism
g
of
M
(see
[
CH99
, Lemma 1, Appendix]
). Now define
\sigma=ig\colon M\to N
. Then
\sigma
is an
\mathcal{L}
-isomorphism, which extends
f
by construction, and therefore must also be an
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]
-isomorphism, as desired.

It now follows that
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is complete and, moreover,
Q
is stably embedded in
T[\mathcal{Q}]
(again by
[
CH99
]
). Finally, suppose
X
is an
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]
-definable subset of
Q^{n}
for some
n\geq 1
. Then by the above,
X(M)
is invariant under automorphisms of
\mathcal{Q}(M)
. By saturation, it follows that
X
is
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-definable.
∎

Proposition 2.8
.

Fix
M\models T[\mathcal{Q}]
,
B\subseteq M
, and
a\in M
. Suppose
c\in Q^{\mathrm{eq}}(M)
is such that
\mathrm{tp}^{T^{\mathrm{eq}}}(a/Bc)\vdash\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/BQ(N))
for some saturated
N\succeq M
with
|N|>|B|
.

(a)

Suppose
E\subseteq Q^{\mathrm{eq}}(M)
is such that any automorphism of
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}(N)
fixing
E
is an automorphism of
Q^{B}_{\textnormal{ind}}(N)
.
Then

\mathrm{tp}^{T^{\mathrm{eq}}}(ac/B)\cup\mathrm{tp}^{\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{eq}}}(c/E)\vdash\mathrm{tp}^{T[\mathcal{Q}]^{\mathrm{eq}}}(ac/B).

(b)

If
E=\mathrm{dcl}^{\mathrm{eq}}(B)\cap Q^{\mathrm{eq}}(M)
then
\mathrm{tp}^{T^{\mathrm{eq}}}(ac/B)\cup\mathrm{tp}^{\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{eq}}}(c/E)\vdash\mathrm{tp}^{T[\mathcal{Q}]^{\mathrm{eq}}}(ac/B)
.

(c)

If
B=\emptyset
or
B
is an elementary substructure of
M
then

\mathrm{tp}^{T}(ac/B)\cup\mathrm{tp}^{\mathcal{Q}}(c/Q(B))\vdash\mathrm{tp}^{T[\mathcal{Q}]}(ac/B).

Proof.

Part
(a)
. It suffices to consider the case where
M
is already saturated with
|M|>|B|
(and so we take
M=N
).

Fix
a^{\prime}c^{\prime}\in M
such that
ac\equiv^{T^{\mathrm{eq}}}_{B}a^{\prime}c^{\prime}
and
c\equiv^{\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{eq}}}_{E}c^{\prime}
. Then by saturation of
\mathcal{Q}(M)
, there is an automorphism
\sigma
of
\mathcal{Q}(M)
sending
c^{\prime}
to
c
and fixing
E
pointwise. By assumption,
\sigma
is an automorphism of
Q^{B}_{\textnormal{ind}}(M)
.
Therefore, in light of Remark
2.3
, we can apply Proposition
2.7
in the setting where
\mathcal{L}
includes constants for
B
, and extend
\sigma
to an
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]
-automorphism of
M
fixing
B
, which we still denote by
\sigma
.

Let
a^{\prime\prime}=\sigma(a^{\prime})
. Then
a^{\prime\prime}c\equiv^{T[\mathcal{Q}]^{\mathrm{eq}}}_{B}a^{\prime}c^{\prime}\equiv^{T^{\mathrm{eq}}}_{B}ac
, and so
\mathrm{tp}^{T^{\mathrm{eq}}}(a^{\prime\prime}/Bc)=\mathrm{tp}^{T^{\mathrm{eq}}}(a/Bc)
. Since
\mathrm{tp}^{T^{\mathrm{eq}}}(a/Bc)\vdash\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/BQ(M))
, we have
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a^{\prime\prime}/BQ(M))=\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/BQ(M))
.
By stable embeddedness of
Q
(even after naming
B
), there exists an
\mathcal{L}
-automorphism
\tau
of
M
that fixes
BQ(M)
pointwise and sends
a^{\prime\prime}
to
a
(see, e.g.,
[
TZ12
, Lemma 10.1.5]
). Note that
\tau
is automatically an
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]
-automorphism as well, and
\tau
fixes
c
since
c\in Q^{\mathrm{eq}}(M)
. So we have
\tau\sigma(a^{\prime}c^{\prime})=ac
and hence
a^{\prime}c^{\prime}\equiv^{T[\mathcal{Q}]^{\mathrm{eq}}}_{B}ac
, as desired.

Part
(b)
. By Remark
2.6
(a)
,
E
satisfies the assumptions in part
(a)
.

Part
(c)
. If
B=\emptyset
then we can clearly take
E=\emptyset
in part
(a)
. If
B\preceq M
then
E=Q(B)
satisfies the assumptions in part
(a)
by Remark
2.6
(b)
.
∎

As a direct consequence of Proposition
2.8
, we now obtain the following corollary, which summarizes two well known results (part
(a)
appears in
[
CS15
, Lemma 46]
; part
(b)
was proved in various settings and stems from
[
Del12
]
). For part
(b)
, to ensure elimination of imaginaries one might need to allow
Q
to be a small union of stably embedded definable sets. The results above can be adapted to this setting; since we will not need this corollary for later work, we leave the proof to the reader.

Corollary 2.9
.

Assume that
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
have quantifier elimination with respect to
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
, respectively.

(a)

Every
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]
-formula
\varphi(x)
is equivalent, modulo
T[\mathcal{Q}]
, to a formula of the form

\square_{1}y_{1}\in Q\ldots\square_{n}y_{n}\in Q\,\psi(x,y),

where
\square_{i}\in\{\forall,\exists\}
and
\psi(x,y)
is a Boolean combination of quantifier-free
\mathcal{L}
-formulas and quantifier-free
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Q}}
-formulas.

(b)

Assume also
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
eliminates imaginaries. Define
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]_{\mathrm{cb}}=\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]\cup\{f_{\varphi}:\varphi\text{ is an $\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]$-formula}\}
,
and let
T[\mathcal{Q}]_{\mathrm{cb}}
be the expansion of
T[\mathcal{Q}]
where we interpret
f_{\varphi}
as in Lemma
2.5
(b). Then
T[\mathcal{Q}]_{\mathrm{cb}}
has quantifier elimination with respect to
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]_{\mathrm{cb}}
.

The next two sections will focus on results showing that various forms of model-theoretic tameness are preserved when passing from
T
and
\mathcal{Q}
to
T[\mathcal{Q}]
(sometimes with further assumptions). So we
end this section with an example showing that stable embeddedness is necessary in general for this kind of preservation result.

Example 2.10
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
consist of two unary relations
P
and
Q
, and a binary relation
E
. Let
T
be the complete theory of the Fraïssé limit of all finite bipartite graphs. Recall that
T
is simple and has quantifier elimination in
\mathcal{L}
. From this one can show that
Q
is not stably embedded, and that the (
\emptyset
-definable) induced structure on
Q
is a pure set. We first note that, given some structure
\mathcal{Q}
expanding
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}
, it need not be the case that
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is complete. For example, let
\mathcal{Q}
add an infinite co-infinite unary predicate
X
on
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}
. Then
T[\mathcal{Q}]
does not decide the sentence saying that there is some point in
P
connected by
E
to every point in
X
.

In light of the previous observation, it is more natural to phrase the question of preserving tameness as a question about models. In particular, if one expands a model
M_{0}
of
T
to an
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]
-structure
M
by expanding
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}(M_{0})
to
\mathcal{Q}
, then does
M
maintain the same tameness present in
T
in
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
? We now show that this is not the case. Let
\mathcal{Q}
be
(\mathbb{Z},+)
, viewed as an expansion of
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}
. Then
T
is simple and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
is stable. Let
M_{0}
be the countable model of
T
. Let
A\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
be an arbitrary infinite co-infinite set, and let
\mathcal{Q}^{*}
be the structure
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
. Since
M_{0}
is universal for infinite bipartite graphs, we can find some point
a\in P(M_{0})
whose
E
-neighborhood
E(a,M_{0})
is infinite and co-infinite. Now expand
M
to an
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]
-structure by naming
\mathcal{Q}=(\mathbb{Z},+)
on
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}(M_{0})
in such a way that
E(a,M_{0})
coincides with
A
. Then
\mathcal{Q}^{*}
is a definable reduct of
M
since
E(a,M_{0})
is
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]
-definable using the parameter
a
.

In conclusion, if
T
and
\mathcal{Q}
are as in the previous paragraph, then any model theoretic complexity that can be obtained when expanding
(\mathbb{Z},+)
by a unary predicate can also be obtained when expanding a model
T
by naming
\mathcal{Q}
on
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}
. So for example, we can obtain the strict order property by using Presburger arithmetic
(\mathbb{Z},+,\mathbb{N})
. More generally, it follows from Theorem
7.16
below that any countable graph can be found as a definable reduct of some
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]
-structure
M\models T[\mathcal{Q}]
obtained as above. Consequently, any countable structure in a finite language can be interpreted in a countable model of
T[\mathcal{Q}]
(e.g., by
[
Hod93
, Theorem 5.5.1]
; one can also extend to countable languages using
[
BZ92
]
).

3.
Preservation of stability, NIP, and NTP
2

3.1.
Stability

Let
T
be a complete stable theory. Fix an
\emptyset
-definable set
Q
in
T
, and an expansion
\mathcal{Q}
of
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}
. Recall that, since
T
is stable,
Q
is automatically stably embedded.

Lemma 3.1
.

Fix a model
M
of
T[\mathcal{Q}]
.

(a)

|S^{T[\mathcal{Q}]}(M)|\leq|S^{T}(M)|^{|T|}\cdot|S^{\mathcal{Q}}(Q(M))|^{|T|}
.

(b)

If
T
is countable and
\omega
-stable, then
|S^{T[\mathcal{Q}]}(M)|\leq|M|\cdot|S^{\mathcal{Q}}(Q(M))|
.

(c)

Given
\lambda>|T[\mathcal{Q}]|
, if
M{\upharpoonright}\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{Q}(M)
are
\lambda
-saturated then so is
M
. Thus if
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
have saturated models of size
\lambda>|T[\mathcal{Q}]|
, then so does
T[\mathcal{Q}]
.

Proof.

Part
(a)
. Let
N\succ M
be a saturated extension. By Lemma
2.4
(a)
and Proposition
2.8
(c)
, for every finite tuple
a\in N
there is some
c_{a}\subseteq Q(N)
with
|c_{a}|\leq|T|
such that
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(ac_{a}/M)\cup\mathrm{tp}^{\mathcal{Q}}(c_{a}/Q(M))\vdash\mathrm{tp}^{T[\mathcal{Q}]}(ac_{a}/M)\vdash\mathrm{tp}^{T[\mathcal{Q}]}(a/M)
. This yields the desired inequality.

Part
(b)
. This follows using the same argument as in part
(a)
, except that by Lemma
2.4
(b)
we may further assume that each
c_{a}
is finite.

Part
(c)
. Let
N\preceq M
with
|N|\leq\lambda
, let
p\in S^{T[\mathcal{Q}]}(N)
, and let
\mathcal{U}
be some monster model of
T[\mathcal{Q}]
. Let
a\models p
in
\mathcal{U}
. By Lemma
2.4
(a)
and Proposition
2.8
(c)
, there is some
c\in Q(\mathcal{U})
such that
|c|\leq|T|
and
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(ac/N)\cup\mathrm{tp}^{\mathcal{Q}}(c/Q(N))\vdash\mathrm{tp}^{T[\mathcal{Q}]}(ac/N)
. Since
\mathcal{Q}(M)
is
\lambda
-saturated, we may find
c^{\prime}\models\mathrm{tp}^{\mathcal{Q}}(c/Q(N))
inside
Q(M)
.
Since
M{\upharpoonright}\mathcal{L}
is
\lambda
-saturated, we can then find
a^{\prime}\in M
such that
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a^{\prime}c^{\prime}/N)=\mathrm{tp}^{T}(ac/N)
. Since we also have
c^{\prime}\models\mathrm{tp}^{\mathcal{Q}}(c/Q(N))
, it follows that
a^{\prime}c^{\prime}\models\mathrm{tp}^{T[\mathcal{Q}]}(ac/N)
and, in particular,
a^{\prime}\models p
.
So we have shown that
M
is
\lambda
-saturated. The final claim now follows using the assumption that
\mathcal{Q}
expands
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}
.
∎

Although it will not be directly used in the subsequent results, we note the following consequence of Lemma
3.1
(a)
.

Remark 3.2
.

Given
\lambda\geq\max\{|T|,|\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})|\}
with
\lambda^{|T|}=\lambda
, if
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
are
\lambda
-stable then
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is also
\lambda
-stable.

We now state the main result of this section, which gives preservation of stability (in various forms) when expanding the induced structure on a definable set. We repeat the standing assumptions for clarity, and we recall again that definable sets in stable theories are automatically stably embedded.

Theorem 3.3
.

Fix a complete theory
T
and an
\emptyset
-definable set
Q
in
T
. Let
\mathcal{Q}
be an arbitrary structure expanding
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}
.

(a)

If
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
are stable, then so is
T[\mathcal{Q}]
.

(b)

If
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
are countable and
\omega
-stable, then so is
T[\mathcal{Q}]
.

(c)

If
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
are superstable, then so is
T[\mathcal{Q}]
.

Proof.

Part
(a)
. Assume that
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
are both stable. We need to check that
|S(M)|\leq|M|^{|T[\mathcal{Q}]|}
for every model
M\models T[\mathcal{Q}]
. This follows easily from Lemma
3.1
(a)
and stability of
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
.

Part
(b)
. This is immediate from Lemma
3.1
(b)
.

Part
(c
). Assume that
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
are both superstable and that
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is not superstable. Then
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is strictly stable by part
(a)
. So by
[
She90
, Theorem II.3.14]
,
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is not
\lambda
-stable for any
\lambda
satisfying
\lambda<\lambda^{\aleph_{0}}
.

Let
\lambda=\beth_{\aleph_{0}}(|T[\mathcal{Q}]|)
. By König’s theorem,
\lambda<\lambda^{\text{cf}(\lambda)}\leq\lambda^{\aleph_{0}}\leq\lambda^{<\lambda}
and obviously
\lambda>|S(T[\mathcal{Q}])|
.
By
[
She90
, Theorem VIII.4.7]
,
T[\mathcal{Q}]
has no saturated model of cardinality
\lambda
. By Lemma
3.1
(c)
, either
T
or
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
has no saturated model of cardinality
\lambda
. Without loss of generality assume that it is
T
; then by
[
She90
, Theorem VIII.4.7]
again,
T
is not
\lambda
-stable. On the other hand,
T
is superstable hence
\lambda
-stable for every
\lambda\geq 2^{|T|}
(
[
She90
, Theorem II.3.14]
), contradiction.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

Part
(a)
of Theorem
3.3
can also be proved in similar fashion to
[
JS20
, Proposition 2.5]
, using the fact that
\varphi(x,y)
has the order property if and only if there is an indiscernible sequence
(a_{i})_{i<\omega 2}
and
b
such that
\varphi(a_{i},b)
holds if and only if
i<\omega
. One must then also prove a corresponding version of
[
JS20
, Lemma 2.2]
.

We take the opportunity now to state a specific application of the previous theorem, since it was one of the seeds that later developed to the results in this section. For motivation, recall that by
[
DG17
, Lemma 2.2]
every (not necessarily pure) field of finite dp-rank eliminates
\exists^{\infty}
, and also that, conjecturally, every strongly dependent pure field is of finite dp-rank
[
HHJ19
, Proposition 3.11]
. During a talk by the third author in GTM Paris, Martin Hils noted that given Theorem
3.3
(b)
, one can construct an
\omega
-stable (expansion of a) field that does not eliminate
\exists^{\infty}
, and thus is strongly dependent but not of finite dp-rank.
5
5

5

DCF
0
is also an example of a strongly dependent field which is not of finite dp-rank.

Corollary 3.5

(Hils)
.

There exists an expansion
\mathcal{K}
of a field
(K,+,\cdot)
such that
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{K})
is
\omega
-stable, but does not eliminate
\exists^{\infty}
.
In particular,
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{K})
is strongly dependent but not of finite dp-rank.

Proof.

Let
K
be an uncountable algebraically closed field and let
I\subseteq K
be a countable indiscernible sequence. By
[
BB00
]
,
(K,+,\cdot,I)
is
\omega
-stable and the induced structure on
I
is a pure set. Let
E
be a new equivalence relation on
I
with exactly one equivalence class of size
n
for every
n<\omega
. By Theorem
3.3
(b)
,
\mathcal{K}=(K,+,\cdot,I,E)
is still
\omega
-stable. On the other hand, it obviously does not eliminate
\exists^{\infty}
and the result follows.
∎

Note that in the previous proof we took an arbitrary uncountable algebraically closed field, expanded it by naming a countable indiscernible sequence equipped with the canonical fcp theory of an equivalence relation, and concluded that the resulting expansion is still
\omega
-stable. After consulting
[
BB00
]
, one can see that the argument works with an arbitrary
\aleph_{1}
-saturated
\omega
-stable structure in place of the field
K
. Theorem
6.10
will provide a closely related (and very general) result about expanding
any
(not necessarily saturated) structure whose theory is superstable of
U
-rank
1
(e.g., a model of ACF).

3.2.
NIP and NTP
2

In this subsection, we briefly discuss analogues of Theorem
3.3
in the context of NIP and NTP
2
theories. As stated in the introduction, the NIP analogue appears in previous literature; see
[
CS15
, Lemma 48]
and
[
JS20
, Proposition 2.4]
.

Fact 3.6

(Chernikov-Simon; Jahnke-Simon)
.

Fix a complete theory
T
and an
\emptyset
-definable set
Q
, which is stably embedded in
T
. Let
\mathcal{Q}
be an arbitrary structure expanding
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}
. If
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
are
\mathrm{NIP}
, then so is
T[\mathcal{Q}]
.

The NTP
2
analogue is essentially proved by Chernikov and Hils in
[
CH14
]
, although not explicitly. So we take the opportunity to spell out the details.

Proposition 3.7
.

Fix a complete theory
T
and an
\emptyset
-definable set
Q
, which is stably embedded in
T
. Let
\mathcal{Q}
be an arbitrary structure expanding
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}
. If
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
are
\mathrm{NTP}_{2}
, then so is
T[\mathcal{Q}]
.

Proof.

As indicated above, the proof is largely a matter of combining several key results from
[
CH14
]
, along with a few basic tools from Section
2
. For a contradiction, assume
T[\mathcal{Q}]
has TP
2
. By
[
CH14
, Lemma 3.9]
, we find an
\mathcal{L}[\mathcal{Q}]
-formula
\varphi(x,y)
and strongly indiscernible array
(a_{ij})_{i,j\in\omega}
witnessing TP
2
(see
[
CH14
, Definition 3.1 and 3.4]
), along with a realization
c\models\{\varphi(x,a_{i0}):i<\omega\}
such that the sequence of rows
(\overline{a}_{i})_{i<\omega}
is indiscernible over
c
. Using Lemma
2.5
(a)
, let
d\in Q
be a tuple such that
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(ca_{00}/d)\vdash\mathrm{tp}^{T}(ca_{00}/Q(N))
for every
N\models T[\mathcal{Q}]
containing
c
and
a_{00}
. Note that, by Proposition
2.7
,
Q
is stably embedded in
T[\mathcal{Q}]
with induced structure
\mathcal{Q}
, which is NTP
2
. Therefore, by
[
CH14
, Lemma 3.8]
, we may assume
d\subseteq a_{00}
.
Now we have
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(ca_{00})\cup\mathrm{tp}^{\mathcal{Q}}(d)\vdash\mathrm{tp}^{T[\mathcal{Q}]}(ca_{00})
by Proposition
2.8
(c)
, and thus
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(c/a_{00})\vdash\mathrm{tp}^{T[\mathcal{Q}]}(c/a_{00})
.
Since
T
is NTP
2
, it follows from
[
CH14
, Lemma 3.11]
that
\{\varphi(x,a_{0j}):j<\omega\}
is consistent, contradicting that
(a_{ij})_{i,j<\omega}
witnesses TP
2
for
\varphi(x,y)
.
∎

4.
Preservation of NSOP
1
and simplicity

4.1.
Preliminaries

Let
T
be a complete theory with monster model
\mathcal{U}
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}
be an invariant ternary relation on small subsets of
\mathcal{U}
. We define the following axioms.

(1)

(normality) If
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}B
then
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}BC
.

(2)

(monotonicity) If
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}BD
then
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}B
.

(3)

(base monotonicity) If
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}BD
then
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{CD}B
.

(4)

(finite character) If
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}B
for all finite
a\subseteq A
, then
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}B
.

(5)

(existence)
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}C
for any
A
and
C
.

(6)

(extension) If
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}B
then for any
D
there is
A^{\prime}\equiv_{BC}A
with
A^{\prime}\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}BD
.

(7)

(symmetry) If
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}B
then
B\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}A
.

(8)

(transitivity) Given
C\subseteq D\subseteq A
, if
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{D}B
and
D\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}B
then
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}B
.

(9)

(local character) For every
A
and
B
there exists
C\subseteq B
such that
\lvert{C}\rvert\leq\lvert{A}\rvert+\lvert{T}\rvert
and
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{C}B
.

(10)

(the independence theorem over models) Let
M
be a small model, and assume
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{M}B
,
C_{1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{M}A
,
C_{2}\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{M}B
, and
C_{1}\equiv_{M}C_{2}
. Then there is a set
C
such that
C\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{M}AB
,
C\equiv_{MA}C_{1}
, and
C\equiv_{MB}C_{2}
.

(11)

(stationarity over models) Let
M
be a small model, and assume
C_{1}\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{M}A
,
C_{2}\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{M}A
, and
C_{1}\equiv_{M}C_{2}
. Then
C_{1}\equiv_{MA}C_{2}
.

We also use the terminology
monotonicity
(resp.,
finite character
,
symmetry
,
existence
,
extension
)
over models
to mean the axiom obtained by restricting to the case when
C
is a small model
M\models T
. By
base monotonicity
(resp.,
transitivity
)
over models
, we mean the axiom obtained by restricting to the case when
C
and
D
are small models
M
and
N
, with
M\subseteq N
.

Remark 4.2
.

Let
\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }
denote forking independence in
T
. Then
\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }
satisfies
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
6
, and
8
(and also satisfies the natural “lefthand” analogues of normality and monotonicity). Moreover, given
A,B,C\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, we have:

(i)

A\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{C}B

~\Leftrightarrow~

A\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{\mathrm{acl}(C)}B

~\Leftrightarrow~

\mathrm{acl}(A)\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{C}B

~\Leftrightarrow~

A\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{C}\mathrm{acl}(B),~
and

(ii)

A\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{C}B

~\Rightarrow~

\mathrm{acl}(A)\cap\mathrm{acl}(B)\subseteq\mathrm{acl}(C)
.

Recall that
T
is simple if and only if forking independence satisfies symmetry. In this case, forking independence coincides with dividing independence, and also satisfies existence, local character, and the independence theorem over models. If
T
is stable, then forking independence satisfies stationarity over models.

Given tuples
a,b\in\mathcal{U}\models T
, and a small model
M
, we say that
\mathrm{tp}(a/Mb)

does not Kim-divide over
M
if for any formula
\varphi(x,b)\in\mathrm{tp}(a/Mb)
and any Morley sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
in a global
M
-invariant extension of
\mathrm{tp}(b/M)
, the partial type
\left\{{\varphi(x,b_{i}):i<\omega}\right\}
is consistent. This leads to the natural analogue of
Kim-forking
, and to the ternary relation of
Kim-independence over models
. By a standard exercise analogous to the case of forking, Kim-forking can be defined by forcing extension:
\mathrm{tp}(a/Mb)
does not Kim-fork over
M
if and only if for every
b^{\prime}\supseteq b
, there exists
a^{\prime}\equiv_{Mb}a
such that
\mathrm{tp}(a^{\prime}/Mb^{\prime})
does not Kim-divide over
M
.

Recall that
T
is NSOP
1
if and only if Kim-independence satisfies symmetry over models. Moreover, if
T
is simple, then Kim-independence and forking independence coincide. See
[
KR20
]
for further details.

Remark 4.3
.

Using arguments similar to the case of forking independence, one can show that Kim-independence satisfies conditions
(i)
and
(ii)
of Remark
4.2
when
C
is a small model
M
. We will only need to use condition
(i)
, which is proved for NSOP
1
theories in
[
KR20
, Corollary 5.17]
. So we sketch the proof for arbitrary theories. Note that since
C=M
is algebraically closed, the analogue of first equivalence in
(i)
is trivial. The analogue of the last equivalence in
(i)
is proved using extension for Kim-forking (exactly as in the case of forking). So we only need to prove the analogue of the second equivalence in
(i)
.

First, if
\mathrm{tp}(\mathrm{acl}(a)/Mb)
does not Kim-fork over
M
then neither does
\mathrm{tp}(a/Mb)
by the proof of
[
KR20
, Corollary 5.17]
. So it remains to show that if
\mathrm{tp}(a/Mb)
does not Kim-fork over
M
, then neither does
\mathrm{tp}(\mathrm{acl}(Ma)/Mb)
. Let us first consider Kim-dividing. If
\mathrm{tp}(\mathrm{acl}(aM)/bM)
Kim-divides over
M
then there is a formula
\varphi(x,b)
and a Morley sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
in
\mathrm{tp}(b/M)
such that for some
c\in\mathrm{acl}(aM)
such that
\varphi(c,b)
, we have that
\bigwedge_{i}\varphi(z,b_{i})
is inconsistent. If
\theta(z,x)\in\mathrm{tp}(ca/M)
is such that
\theta(M,e)
is always finite, then
\bigwedge_{i}\exists z\varphi(z,b_{i})\wedge\theta(z,x)
is also inconsistent, hence
\mathrm{tp}(a/bM)
Kim-divides. If
\mathrm{tp}(a/Mb)
does not Kim-fork over
M
then, for every
b^{\prime}\supseteq b
, there is an
a^{\prime}\equiv_{Mb}a
such that
\mathrm{tp}(a^{\prime}/Mb^{\prime})
does not Kim-divide over
M
. But then
\mathrm{tp}(\mathrm{acl}(Ma^{\prime})/Mb^{\prime})
does not Kim-divide over
M
and
\mathrm{acl}(Ma^{\prime})\equiv_{Mb}\mathrm{acl}(Ma)
, so
\mathrm{tp}(\mathrm{acl}(Ma)/Mb)
does not Kim-fork over
M
.

We will make use of the following observation.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
T_{0}
be a simple reduct of
T
. For any
M\models T
and
a,b\in\mathcal{U}
, if
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/Mb)
does not Kim-divide over
M
(in the sense of
T
), then
\mathrm{tp}^{T_{0}}(a/Mb)
does not fork over
M
(in the sense of
T_{0}
).

Proof.

Let
p
be a global extension of
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(b/M)
, which is finitely satisfiable in
M
, and
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
a Morley sequence in
p
over
M
, by which we mean that
b_{n}\models p{\upharpoonright}M(b_{i})_{i<n}
for all
n<\omega
. Let
\varphi(x,b)\in\mathrm{tp}^{T_{0}}(a/Mb)
. We show that
\varphi(x,b)
does not fork over
M
(in the sense of
T_{0}
). By simplicity of
T_{0}
, it is enough to check that
\varphi(x,b)
does not divide over
M
. As
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/Mb)
does not Kim-divide over
M
and
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
is a Morley sequence in the
M
-invariant type
p
, the partial type
\{\varphi(x,b_{i}):i<\omega\}
is consistent. Since
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
is still a Morley sequence in the sense of
T_{0}
(note that the restriction of
p
to
T_{0}
is still finitely satisfiable in
M
), it then follows from Kim’s Lemma that
\varphi(x,b)
does not divide over
M
.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

In the previous proof, the only facts used about
T_{0}
are Kim’s Lemma (dividing with respect to one indiscernible sequence is equivalent to dividing with respect to any indiscernible sequence) and the equivalence of forking and dividing. Both of these facts have analogues for Kim-independence in the setting of NSOP
1
theories
[
KR20
]
. Thus the proof of Lemma
4.4
also yields that if
T_{0}
is an
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
reduct of
T
, and
\mathrm{tp}^{T}(a/Mb)
does not Kim-divide over
M
, then
\mathrm{tp}^{T_{0}}(a/Mb)
does not Kim-fork over
M
. We also note that if
T_{0}
is assumed to be stable with elimination of imaginaries, then Lemma
4.4
has a generalization over
\mathrm{acl}
-closed sets (this is well-known in the literature; see, e.g.,
[
BMPW15
, Lemma 2.1]
).

Recall that in
[
KP97
]
, Kim and Pillay characterized simple theories using the existence of invariant ternary relation satisfying various axioms, which characterize the behavior of forking independence in the simple setting. An analogous treatment of NSOP
1
theories was developed in the work of Chernikov and Ramsey
[
CR16
, Proposition 5.8]
and of Kaplan and Ramsey
[
KR20
, Theorem 9.1]
. This led to a characterization of Kim-independence in NSOP
1
theories which, while similar to the Kim-Pillay theorem, was not a direct generalization in the strict sense. In
[
DK22
]
, Dobrowolski and Kamsma extended key results on NSOP
1
theories to the setting of
positive logic
. In doing so, they gave an axiomatization of Kim-independence over models in NSOP
1
theories which, when translated to the first-order setting, yields a more direct correspondence to the result of Kim and Pillay. In particular, their axiomatization can be obtained from
[
KP97
, Theorem 4.2]
by specializing all axioms to be over models, removing
3
, and replacing
9
by the following variation, which we refer to as
4.1
.

(chain local character) Let
a
be a finite tuple and
\kappa>\lvert{T}\rvert
a regular cardinal. For every continuous chain
(M_{i})_{i<\kappa}
of models with
\lvert{M_{i}}\rvert<\kappa
for all
i<\kappa
and
M=\bigcup_{i<\kappa}M_{i}
, there is
j<\kappa
such that
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{M_{j}}M
.

Fact 4.6

(Chernikov-Ramsey; Kaplan-Ramsey; Dobrowolski-Kamsma)
.

A complete theory
T
is
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
if and only if there is an invariant ternary relation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}
on small subsets of
\mathcal{U}
, which satisfies
7
over models,
5
over models,
4
over models,
2
over models,
8
over models,
6
over models,
10
over models, and
4.1
.
Moreover, in this case
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}
is Kim-independence over models.

Proof.

We need to check that
[
DK22
, Theorem 9.1]
implies the above. Let
T
be any theory and
T^{\mathrm{mor}}
be its Morleyization viewed as a theory in positive logic. Then
T^{\mathrm{mor}}
is
boolean
in the sense of
[
DK22
, Definition 2.13]
, hence in particular, Hausdorff, semi-Hausdorff, and thick (see also Remark 2.14). Thus
T
is
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
if and only if
T^{\mathrm{mor}}
is
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
in positive logic (
[
DK22
, Definition 2.26, Remark 2.27]
). Obviously, models of
T^{\mathrm{mor}}
are existentially closed. Moreover, having the same Lascar strong type in the positive sense for
T^{\mathrm{mor}}
is equivalent to having the same Lascar strong type in the first order sense in a model of
T
(by
[
DK22
, Remark 3.4]
, as
T^{\mathrm{mor}}
is semi-Hausdorff). When the base set is a model, this coincides with having the same type in
T
. Finally, we need to check that the positive logic definition of Kim-dividing in
T^{\mathrm{mor}}
is equivalent to the first-order logic definition in
T
. This follows from Kim’s Lemma (
[
DK22
, Proposition 4.3]
) since Lstp-invariance and invariance coincide over models (see
[
DK22
, Definition 4.1]
).
∎

4.2.
Canonical bases and algebraic embeddedness

Let
T
be a complete
\mathcal{L}
-theory, and fix an
\emptyset
-definable stably embedded set
Q
. Let
\mathcal{U}
be a sufficiently saturated monster model of
T
. We identify
Q
with
Q(\mathcal{U})
.

Recall that the main goal of this section is to prove preservation results for simplicity and NSOP
1
, when enriching
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}
by some further structure. For these tameness properties, we will require an additional assumption on
Q
(among other things). Thus the purpose of this subsection is to define this assumption, establish some basic facts, and point to existing examples from the literature.

Let
Q^{\mathrm{eq}}
denote
Q^{\mathrm{eq}}(\mathcal{U})
(as defined before Lemma
2.5
).
We define an operator on subsets of
\mathcal{U}
, which will act as a canonical base for types over
Q
.

Definition 4.7
.

Given
A\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, set
\overline{A}=\mathrm{dcl}^{\mathrm{eq}}(A)\cap Q^{\mathrm{eq}}
.

Remark 4.8
.

Note that if
A\equiv^{T}_{\overline{A}}A^{\prime}
then
\overline{A^{\prime}}=\overline{A}
(for a given choice of enumeration, induced by that of
A
and
A^{\prime}
).

Lemma 4.9
.

If
A\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, then
\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(A)\cap Q^{\mathrm{eq}}=\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(\overline{A})\cap Q^{\mathrm{eq}}
. In particular, if
B\subseteq A
, then
\overline{\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(B\overline{A})}=\mathrm{acl}_{T}^{\mathrm{eq}}(\overline{A})\cap Q^{\mathrm{eq}}
.

Proof.

It is immediate that
\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(\overline{A})\cap Q^{\mathrm{eq}}\subseteq\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(A)\cap Q^{\mathrm{eq}}
. Conversely, consider
e\in\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}(A)\cap Q^{\mathrm{eq}}
. Then
e
is contained in a finite
A
-definable subset
C
of (some sort in)
Q^{\mathrm{eq}}
. By Remark
2.6
(a)
,
C
is
\overline{A}
-definable, and thus
e\in\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(\overline{A})
.

As for the second equality, we have
\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(\overline{A})\cap Q^{\mathrm{eq}}\subseteq\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(B\overline{A})\cap Q^{\mathrm{eq}}=\overline{\mathrm{acl}_{T}^{\mathrm{eq}}(B\overline{A})}
. Conversely, since
B\overline{A}\subseteq\mathrm{dcl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(A)
, we have

\overline{\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(B\overline{A})}=\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(B\overline{A})\cap Q^{\mathrm{eq}}\subseteq\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(A)\cap Q^{\mathrm{eq}}=\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(\overline{A})\cap Q^{\mathrm{eq}},

as needed.
∎

Throughout this section,
\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }
denotes forking independence in
T^{\mathrm{eq}}
.
We assume that every
A\subseteq\mathcal{U}
is an extension base for
\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }
; this holds in particular if
T
is simple.

Lemma 4.10
.

If
A\subseteq\mathcal{U}
then
A\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{\overline{A}}C
for any
C\subseteq Q^{\mathrm{eq}}
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathrm{tp}^{\mathrm{eq}}(A/\overline{A})\vdash\mathrm{tp}(A/Q)
by Lemma
2.5
(c)
. Since
Q^{\mathrm{eq}}\subseteq\mathrm{dcl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(Q)
and
A\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{\overline{A}}\overline{A}
, this yields the claim. ∎

Definition 4.11
.

We say that
Q
is
algebraically embedded
in
T
if, for any
A,B,C\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, if
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}^{T}_{C}B
and
C
contains a model, then
\overline{ABC}\subseteq\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(\overline{AC},\overline{BC})
.

Remark 4.12
.

The only place where we use algebraic embeddedeness with
C
not
equal
to a model is in the proof of Lemma
4.25
. In that case,
C=MQ(N)
for some
M\prec\mathcal{N}\prec\mathcal{U}
. That being said, we note that in all of the examples we will consider below, algebraic embeddedness holds with
C
being arbitrary. Thus the reader might argue that it would be more natural to phrase Definition
4.11
with
C
arbitrary, leading to a stronger condition. However, one can construct examples (even in stable theories) where
Q
is algebraically embedded as defined above, but the stronger condition with
C
arbitrary does not hold (briefly: consider a
2
-sorted structure with
(\mathbb{Q},+)
in one sort and
\mathbb{Q}
as a pure set in the other, with the natural action of the former sort on the latter; let
Q
be the pure set sort). As a matter of fact, we have so far been unable to find an example of a simple (or even stable) theory and a stably embedded definable set that is not algebraically embedded (as defined above).

Remark 4.13
.

(a)

Q
is algebraically embedded in
T
if and only if, for any
A,B,C\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, if
C
contains a model and
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}^{T}_{C}B
then
\overline{ABC}\subseteq\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{Q_{\textnormal{ind}}}(\overline{AC},\overline{BC})
.
So, loosely speaking, any element of
Q^{\mathrm{eq}}
that is algebraic over parameters
ab
with
a\in A
and
b\in B
, must also be algebraic over parameters
a^{\prime}b^{\prime}
with
a^{\prime}\in A
,
b^{\prime}\in B
, and
a^{\prime}b^{\prime}
from
Q^{\mathrm{eq}}
. This observation is behind our choice of the terminology.

(b)

Suppose
\operatorname{Th}(Q_{\textnormal{ind}})
has geometric elimination of imaginaries. Given
A\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, let
\overline{A}^{r}=\mathrm{acl}_{T}(A)\cap Q
. Then
\overline{A}\subseteq\mathrm{acl}_{T}^{\mathrm{eq}}(\overline{A}^{r})
. So
Q
is algebraically embedded in
T
if and only if for any
A,B,C\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, if
C
contains a model and
A\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{C}B
then
\overline{ABC}^{r}\subseteq\mathrm{acl}_{T}(\overline{AC}^{r},\overline{BC}^{r})
.
