by Corollary
4.34
. Since
\overline{ab}\subseteq\overline{g}
, we have
\overline{e}\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{Q}$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{\overline{M}}\overline{ab}
. Recall that
e_{1}
enumerates
\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(M\overline{c_{1}})
and thus
\overline{c}_{1}\subseteq\overline{e}
. Since
c_{1}e_{1}\equiv^{T}c^{\prime}_{1}e\equiv^{T}ce
, it follows that
\overline{c}\subseteq\overline{e}
. Therefore
\overline{c}\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{Q}$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{\overline{M}}\overline{ab}
.
\dashv_{\text{\scriptsize{claim}}}

Finally, we prove
c\equiv^{T[\mathcal{Q}]}_{a}c_{1}
and
c\equiv^{T[\mathcal{Q}]}_{b}c_{2}
. Recall from the proof of Claim 3 that
\overline{c}\subseteq e
. Since
c\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{M}a
(by Claim 3), and
Q
is algebraically embedded in
T
, we have

\overline{ca}\subseteq\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(\overline{c},\overline{a})\subseteq\mathrm{acl}^{\mathrm{eq}}_{T}(e\overline{a})\subseteq a^{\prime}.

Since
c\equiv^{T}_{a^{\prime}}c^{\prime}_{1}
, it follows that
\overline{ca}=\overline{c^{\prime}_{1}a}
. So we have
c\equiv^{T}_{a}c^{\prime}_{1}
and
\overline{ca}=\overline{c^{\prime}_{1}a}
which, by Lemma
4.19
(c), yields
c\equiv^{T[\mathcal{Q}]}_{a}c^{\prime}_{1}
. Since
c^{\prime}_{1}\equiv^{T[\mathcal{Q}]}_{a}c_{1}
, we get
c\equiv^{T[\mathcal{Q}]}_{a}c_{1}
. Finally,
c\equiv^{T[\mathcal{Q}]}_{b}c_{2}
follows from a similar argument.
∎

Corollary 4.36
.

Assume that
T
is simple with elimination of hyperimaginaries and
Q
is algebraically embedded in
T
. Then
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is simple if and only if
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
is simple. Moreover, in this case forking independence over models in
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is given by

A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{M}B\makebox[36.135pt]{$\Leftrightarrow$}A\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{M}B\makebox[28.90755pt]{and}\overline{AM}\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{Q}$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{\overline{M}}~\overline{BM},

where
\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }
and
\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{Q}$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }
are forking independence in
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
, respectively.

Proof.

Once again, if
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is simple then so is
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
. So assume
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
is simple.
Let
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}
be as defined above. By Theorem
4.35
,
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is NSOP
1
and
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}
coincides with Kim-independence over models. Therefore, to prove the corollary, it suffices by
[
KR20
, Propositions 8.4 and 8.8]
to show that
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}
satisfies base monotonicity over models. So fix small models
M\subseteq N
, and suppose
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{M}BN
. Then
A\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{M}BN
, and so
A\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{N}B
by base monotonicity for
\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }
. We also have
\overline{AM}\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{Q}$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{\overline{M}}\overline{BN}
. So
\overline{AM}\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{Q}$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{\overline{N}}\overline{B}
by base monotonicity for
\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{Q}$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }
. As
AM\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{M}N
and
Q
is algebraically embedded in
T
, we have
\overline{AN}\subseteq\mathrm{acl}_{\mathcal{Q}}(\overline{AM},\overline{N})
, and thus
\overline{AN}\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{Q}$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{\overline{N}}\overline{B}
(using Remark
4.2
(i)
). Altogether,
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}_{N}B
, as desired.
∎

Remark 4.37
.

Let us discuss the extra assumptions present in Theorem
4.35
. The reader will have noticed that many steps of the proof involving forking calculus require algebraic embeddedness of
Q
.
As for simplicity of
T
, it is used in the following places:

(1)

We regularly use that all sets are extension bases for nonforking, e.g., in the proofs of Lemmas
4.10
and
4.25
.

(2)

The proof of
6
for
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}
uses
3
(on the left) and
8
(on the right) for
\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }
over arbitrary base sets.

(3)

The proof of
4.1
for
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}
uses
9
and
3
for
\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }
over arbitrary base sets.

(4)

The proof of
10
over models for
\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}
uses
3
for
\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }
.

The remaining extra assumption, namely elimination of hyperimaginaries for
T
, could likely be avoided with more work. For example, it is very probable that all of what we do extends to continuous logic where the distinction between imaginaries and hyperimaginaries is irrelevant, although it might be tedious to develop the necessary tools. On the other hand, recall that elimination of hyperimaginaries is known if
T
is stable
[
PP87
]
or supersimple
[
BPW01
, Corollary 5.9]
. Indeed, it is conjectured that every simple theory has this property.

Altogether, it remains an open question whether, assuming
Q
is algebraically embedded in
T
, we have that NSOP
1
and/or simplicity pass from
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
to
T[\mathcal{Q}]
. In the NSOP
1
case, it would also be natural to work with a weaker form of algebraic embeddedness in which
\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }
is Kim-independence in
T
. That being said, we do not know whether algebraic embeddedness (in any form) is necessary in Theorem
4.35
and Corollary
4.36
. Indeed, an interesting question for future work is whether preservation results for simplicity and NSOP
1
can be obtained using a combinatorial approach along the lines of what is done for NIP by Jahnke and Simon
[
JS20
]
and for NTP
2
by Chernikov and Hils
[
CH14
]
(as mentioned in Remark
3.4
, such an approach also works for preserving stability). On the other hand, this method would not directly lead to a characterization of forking/Kim-independence in
T[\mathcal{Q}]
. Indeed, note that if
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
are both stable then, assuming
Q
is algebraically embedded in
T
, Corollary
4.36
provides a characterization of forking independence over models in
T[\mathcal{Q}]
, which does not immediately follow from the proof of Theorem
3.3
.

5.
Connection to interpolative fusions

In this section, we summarize the overlap between the previous results and the work of Kruckman, Tran, and Walsberg
[
KTW21
,
KTW22
]
on
interpolative fusions
. We first show that any theory
T[\mathcal{Q}]
defined as in Section
2
can be built as an interpolative fusion of two theories over a common base.

Let
T
be a complete
\mathcal{L}
-theory, and fix an
\emptyset
-definable stably embedded set
Q
. Let
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}
denote the
\emptyset
-induced structure on
Q
. Without loss of generality, assume
\mathcal{L}
contains a relation for
Q
and relations
R_{\varphi}(x)
for any
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(x)
, and
T
contains the sentence
\forall x(R_{\varphi}(x)\leftrightarrow(\varphi(x)\wedge\bigwedge Q(x_{i})))
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{\cap}=\{Q\}\cup\{R_{\varphi}:\varphi(x)\text{ an $\mathcal{L}$-formula}\}
, and let
T_{\cap}
be the
\mathcal{L}_{\cap}
-reduct of
T
. So
T_{\cap}
is the theory of
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}
on
Q
and a pure set on
\neg Q
.

Now let
\mathcal{Q}
be some arbitrary expansion of
\mathcal{Q}_{\textnormal{ind}}
. Without loss of generality, we can view
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
as expanding
T_{\cap}
in a language
\mathcal{L}_{2}\supseteq\mathcal{L}_{\cap}
, with
\mathcal{L}_{2}\cap\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{\cap}
. In other words, add to
\mathcal{Q}
a pure set for
\neg Q
with no interaction to
\mathcal{Q}
. Let
\mathcal{L}_{1}=\mathcal{L}
and
T_{1}=T
. Then we have
T_{1}\cap T_{2}=T_{\cap}
, and
T_{1}\cup T_{2}=T[\mathcal{Q}]
. In particular,
T_{1}\cup T_{2}
is a complete theory.

Proposition 5.1
.

Any model of
T[\mathcal{Q}]=T_{1}\cup T_{2}
is interpolative (over
T_{\cap}
).

Proof.

Fix
M\models T[\mathcal{Q}]
. For
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
X_{i}\subseteq M^{n}
be an
\mathcal{L}_{i}(M)
-definable set, and assume
X_{1}\cap X_{2}=\emptyset
. We need to find an
\mathcal{L}_{\cap}(M)
-definable set
Y\subseteq M^{n}
such that
X_{1}\subseteq Y
and
X_{2}\cap Y=\emptyset
. Given
I\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
, let
P_{I}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}P_{I,i}
where
P_{I,i}
is
Q(M)
if
i\in I
and
\neg Q(M)
otherwise. Then
(P_{I})_{I}
forms a partition of
M^{n}
into
\mathcal{L}_{\cap}
-definable sets. So it suffices to fix some
I
, and find an
\mathcal{L}_{\cap}(M)
-definable set
Y_{I}\subseteq P_{I}
such that
X_{1}\cap P_{I}\subseteq Y_{I}
and
X_{2}\cap Y_{I}=\emptyset
(since we may then take
Y=\bigcup_{I}Y_{I}
). So fix some
I
. After permuting coordinates, we may assume without loss of generality, that
I
is an initial segment of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Write
P_{I}=A\times B
where
A=Q(M)^{|I|}
and
B=(\neg Q(M))^{n-|I|}
.

For
t\in\{1,2\}
, let
X^{\prime}_{t}=X_{t}\cap P_{I}
. We want to find an
\mathcal{L}_{\cap}(M)
-definable set
Y_{I}\subseteq P_{I}
such that
X^{\prime}_{1}\subseteq Y_{I}
and
X^{\prime}_{2}\cap Y_{I}=\emptyset
.
Since
M|_{\mathcal{L}_{2}}
is a pure set outside of
Q
, and
X_{2}
is
\mathcal{L}_{2}(M)
-definable, we may write
X^{\prime}_{2}=\bigcup_{j\in J}C_{j}\times D_{j}
where each
C_{j}
is an
\mathcal{L}_{2}(M)
-definable subset of
A
and each
D_{j}
is an
\mathcal{L}_{\cap}(M)
-definable subset of
B
(more specifically, each
D_{j}
is the trace on
B
by some formula in the language of equality). For each
j\in J
, we will find an
\mathcal{L}_{\cap}(M)
-definable set
Y_{I,j}\subseteq P_{I}
such that
X^{\prime}_{1}\subseteq Y_{I,j}
and
(C_{j}\times D_{j})\cap Y_{I,j}=\emptyset
; and then let
Y_{I}=\bigcap_{j\in J}Y_{I,j}
.

Fix some
j\in J
. Define
V\coloneqq A\times(B\backslash D_{j})
, and note that
V
is an
\mathcal{L}_{\cap}(M)
-definable subset of
A\times B
. Then
X^{\prime\prime}_{1}\coloneqq X^{\prime}_{1}\backslash V
is
\mathcal{L}(M)
-definable, and so we may fix an
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi(x,x^{\prime},y)
, where
x=(x_{i})_{i\in I}
,
x^{\prime}=(x_{i})_{i\not\in I}
, and
X^{\prime\prime}_{1}
is defined by
\varphi(x,x^{\prime},c)
for some
c\in M
. Let
\psi(x,y)
be
\exists x^{\prime}\varphi(x,x^{\prime},y)
. Then
\psi(x,c)
defines the projection of
X^{\prime\prime}_{1}
to the coordinates in
I
, which we denote by
W
. Note that
W\subseteq A
. By stable embeddedness, there is an
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\theta(x,z)
and some
d\in Q(M)
such that
\theta(Q(M),d)=\psi(Q(M),c)
. Therefore
W
is
\mathcal{L}_{\cap}(M)
-definable via the formula
R_{\theta}(x,d)\wedge\bigwedge_{i\in I}Q(x_{i})
.

Define
Y_{I,j}\coloneqq(W\times B)\cup V
. Then
Y_{I,j}
is
\mathcal{L}_{\cap}(M)
-definable. We show that
X^{\prime}_{1}\subseteq Y_{I,j}
and
(C_{j}\times D_{j})\cap Y_{I,j}=\emptyset
. So first fix some
(a,b)\in X^{\prime}_{1}
. If
(a,b)\in V
then
(a,b)\in Y_{I,j}
. So assume
(a,b)\not\in V
. Then
(a,b)\in X^{\prime\prime}_{1}
, and so
a\in W
, hence
(a,b)\in W\times B\subseteq Y_{I,j}
.

Finally, we show
(C_{j}\times D_{j})\cap Y_{I,j}=\emptyset
. Note that
(C_{j}\times D_{j})\cap V=\emptyset
by definition of
V
. So it suffices to show
(C_{j}\times D_{j})\cap(W\times B)=\emptyset
. For this, we show that
C_{j}\cap W=\emptyset
. Toward a contradiction, suppose there is some
a\in C_{j}\cap W
. Since
a\in W
, there is some
b\in B
such that
(a,b)\in X^{\prime\prime}_{1}
. By definition of
X^{\prime\prime}_{1}
, it follows that
(a,b)\not\in V
and so, in particular,
b\in D_{j}
by definition of
V
. But then
(a,b)\in X^{\prime\prime}_{1}\cap(C_{j}\times D_{j})\subseteq X_{1}\cap X_{2}
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Next we directly translate two main preservation results proved in
[
KTW22
]
for
arbitrary
interpolative fusions to the setting of
T[\mathcal{Q}]
.

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
T
be a complete
\mathcal{L}
-theory,
Q
an
\emptyset
-definable stably embedded set, and
\mathcal{Q}
an expansion of
Q_{\textnormal{ind}}
.

(a)

Suppose
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
are
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
, and
\operatorname{Th}(Q_{\textnormal{ind}})
is stable with trivial forking. Then
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
.

(b)

Suppose
\operatorname{Th}(Q_{\textnormal{ind}})
is stable, and both
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
are simple and disintegrated relative to
\operatorname{Th}(Q_{\textnormal{ind}})
.
7
7

7

In
[
KTW22
]
, a theory
T_{1}
is called
disintegrated relative to
a reduct
T_{0}
if, for any
M\models T_{1}
and
A\subseteq M
,
\mathrm{acl}_{T_{1}}(A)=\mathrm{acl}_{T_{0}}(\bigcup_{a\in A}\mathrm{acl}_{T_{1}}(a))
(where
a
ranges over
singletons
).
Then
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is simple.

Proof.

In light of Proposition
5.1
, parts
(a)
and
(b)
follow from
[
KTW22
, Corollary 4.8]
and
[
KTW22
, Theorem 4.11]
, respectively.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

(a)

Corollary
5.2
(a)
is orthogonal to our preservation result for
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
(Theorem
4.35
) since we do not need to assume
\operatorname{Th}(Q_{\textnormal{ind}})
is stable with trivial forking, but we do need to assume
T
is simple and eliminates hyperimaginaries (hence
\operatorname{Th}(Q_{\textnormal{ind}})
is simple as well) and
Q
is algebraically embedded. However, the main preservation result for
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
in
[
KTW22
]
actually works under the weaker assumption that Kim-independence in
T
and
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{Q})
both satisfy the “
\operatorname{Th}(Q_{\textnormal{ind}})
-generic independence theorem” (see
[
KTW22
, Theorem 4.6]
). Evidently, this condition holds in all known examples of an
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
theory and a stable reduct. Thus, conjecturally, one only needs to assume
\operatorname{Th}(Q_{\textnormal{ind}})
is stable in part
(a)
in order to preserve NSOP
1
using interpolative fusions.

(b)

Corollary
5.2
(b)
is also orthogonal to our preservation result for simplicity (Corollary
4.36
) since we do not need to assume stability of
\operatorname{Th}(Q_{\textnormal{ind}})
or disintegration, but we do need to assume
T
is simple with elimination of hyperimaginaries and
Q
is algebraically embedded. However, if
T
is disintegrated relative to
\operatorname{Th}(Q_{\textnormal{ind}})
, and
\operatorname{Th}(Q_{\textnormal{ind}})
has geometric elimination of imaginaries, then
Q
is automatically algebraically embedded in
T
(e.g., it is easy to check the characterization in Remark
4.13
(b)
).

There are a number of other preservation theorems in
[
KTW22
]
which, when applied to the setting of
T[\mathcal{Q}]
, yield special cases of what we have proved above. For example, NIP, stability, superstability, and
\omega
-stability can be preserved in general interpolative fusions satisfying additional restrictions on
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
(see
[
KTW22
, Section 4.1]
). In fact, when working with
arbitrary
interpolative fusions, one does not expect unconditional preservation results. For example, it is possible to interpret the generic triangle-free graph in the interpolative fusion of two simple theories (see
[
KTW22
, Section 5.2]
). Further examples are constructed in
[
KTW22
]
, which illustrate that many of their preservation results are sharp for general interpolative fusions. However, it is worth pointing out that by
[
KTW22
, Corollary 4.10]
, if the interpolative fusion of two stable theories exists and is NIP, then it must be stable. Combined with preservation of NIP in
T[\mathcal{Q}]
(Fact
3.6
), this gives an alternative proof for preservation of stability in
T[\mathcal{Q}]
(Theorem
3.3
(a)
).

6.
Tame expansions of weakly minimal structures

The rest of this article is devoted to applications of the preservation results proved above. Our focus will now shift to expansions of specific
structures
rather than theories. Given a structure
\mathcal{M}
, we will use the previous theorems to build expansions of
\mathcal{M}
that simultaneously introduce new model theoretic complexity, while also preserving some desired level of tameness. The general approach will be to add new structure on a (definable, stably embedded) set
Q
in
\mathcal{M}
. This presents the main obstacle of understanding the induced structure on
Q
.
Thus we will first investigate subsets of structures that are not necessarily definable, but do admit very trivial induced structure. Then, in order to apply our preservation results, we will name such a set by a new predicate. Of course, this risks losing whatever model-theoretic tameness we might have had at the start. Therefore, we will need to work in a setting where one can freely name new predicates with sufficiently trivial induced structure, without introducing too much complexity. Moreover, there is also the issue of stable embeddedness for the named predicate. By known results (see Fact
6.8
), superstable theories of
U
-rank
1
provide setting in which we can resolve all of these issues. Thus the main results of this section will be about expansions of structures that are
weakly minimal
(i.e., superstable of
U
-rank
1
).

6.1.
Eventually indiscernible sequences

Recall that our focus is now on structures rather than theories. So throughout this subsection, we fix an arbitrary
\mathcal{L}
-structure
\mathcal{M}
with universe
M
.

The goal of this subsection is to identify subsets of
M
that are both easy to find and also admit induced structure that is as trivial as possible. In the stable setting, a typical example would be a set enumerated by an indiscernible sequence. However, there is no guarantee that such a sequence can be found inside a
particular structure
. For example, in
(\mathbb{Z},+)
there are no non-constant indiscernible sequences. Thus we will work instead with “eventually” indiscernible sequences.

Definition 6.1
.

A sequence
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
from
M
is
eventually indiscernible over
A\subseteq M
if for any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, there is some
k_{\varphi}>0
such that if
k_{\varphi}<i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}
and
k_{\varphi}<j_{1}<\ldots<j_{n}
then
\mathcal{M}\models\varphi(a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{n}})\leftrightarrow\varphi(a_{j_{1}},\ldots,a_{j_{n}})
.

Unlike the situation with indiscernible sequences, there is always a plentiful supply of eventually indiscernible sequences inside a given structure in a countable language. In particular, we have the following fact, whose proof is a standard application of Ramsey’s Theorem (see
[
Gan22
, Section 4]
and
[
Sim15
, Section 2.1]
for related discussion).

Fact 6.2
.

Suppose
\mathcal{L}
is countable and
A
is a countable subset of
M
. Then any infinite sequence from
M
has a subsequence that is eventually indiscernible over
A
.

If
\mathcal{L}
is countable then, using the previous fact, one can construct an infinite sequence
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
in
M
which is eventually indiscernible over itself, or even over all of
M
(if
M
is countable). This provides a stark contrast to the behavior of indiscernible sequences.

We will soon analyze the induced structure on eventually indiscernible sequences. In general, this structure may include a total order; but of course in a stable context this does not happen.

Fact 6.3
.

Assume
\mathcal{M}
is stable and
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
is a sequence from
M
, which is eventually indiscernible over some
A\subseteq M
. Then
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
is
eventually totally indiscernible over
A
, i.e., for any
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, there is some
k_{\varphi}>0
such that if
i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}>k_{\varphi}
are pairwise distinct and
j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}>k_{\varphi}
are pairwise distinct then
\mathcal{M}\models\varphi(a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{n}})\leftrightarrow\varphi(a_{j_{1}},\ldots,a_{j_{n}})
.

Proof.

This is essentially identical to the argument that an indiscernible sequence in a stable theory is totally indiscernible.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

Like with indiscernibility, the definition of eventual indiscernibility can be localized to a particular set
\Delta
of formulas
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. So we note that in this more general setting, the analogue of the previous fact still holds as long as every formula in
\Delta
is stable (under any bi-partitioning of the free variables).

Lemma 6.5
.

Suppose
Q\subseteq M
is enumerated by an injective sequence
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
, and fix
A\subseteq M
such that
a_{i}\in A
for all
i<\omega
. Then
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
is eventually totally indiscernible over
A
if and only if
Q^{\mathcal{M}_{A}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is interdefinable with
(Q,=)
.

Proof.

Suppose first that
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
is eventually totally indiscernible over
A
. Given an
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, let
R_{\varphi}=\{\bar{d}\in Q^{n}:\mathcal{M}\models\varphi(\bar{d})\}
. We want to show that each
R_{\varphi}
is definable in
(Q,=)
. Let
k=k_{\varphi}<\omega
be given by the assumption that
(a_{i})_{i<\omega}
is eventually totally indiscernible. Let
Y_{\varphi}
be the set of tuples
\bar{d}\in R_{\varphi}
such the coordinates of
\bar{d}
are pairwise distinct, and no coordinate of
\bar{d}
is in
a_{\leq k}
. Set
Z_{\varphi}=R_{\varphi}\backslash Y_{\varphi}
. We show that
Y_{\varphi}
and
Z_{\varphi}
are both definable in
(Q,=)
.

By choice of
k
,
Y_{\varphi}
is either empty or consists of all injective tuples from
Q^{n}
such that no coordinate is in
a_{\leq k}
. In either case,
Y_{\varphi}
is definable in
(Q,=)
. As for
Z_{\varphi}
, note that any tuple in
Z_{\varphi}
either has two coordinates that are equal, or has some coordinate from
a_{\leq k}
. Since
a_{\leq k}\subseteq A
, it follows that
Z_{\varphi}
is definable in the structure on
Q
induced from
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas with fewer than
n
free variables. Arguing by induction on
n
, we conclude that
Z_{\varphi}
is definable in
(Q,=)
.

Conversely, suppose that the
Q^{\mathcal{M}_{A}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is interdefinable with
\mathcal{Q}\coloneqq(Q,=)
. Fix an
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. Then there is some formula
\psi(\bar{x},\bar{y})
in the language of equality, and some tuple
\bar{b}
from
Q^{\bar{y}}
, such that given
\bar{d}\in Q^{n}
, we have
\mathcal{M}\models\varphi(\bar{d})
if and only if
\mathcal{Q}\models\psi(\bar{d},\bar{b})
. Since
\mathcal{Q}\preceq\mathcal{M}|_{=}
, it follows that if
\bar{d}\in Q^{n}
then
\mathcal{M}\models\varphi(\bar{d})\leftrightarrow\psi(\bar{d},\bar{b})
. Let
k=k_{\varphi}<\omega
be such that
a_{i}
does not appear in
\bar{b}
for all
i>k
. Then
(a_{i})_{i>k}
is totally indiscernible over
\bar{b}
with respect to the language of equality. So for any pairwise distinct
i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}>k
and pairwise distinct
j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}>k
, we have

\mathcal{M}\models\psi(a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{n}},\bar{b})\leftrightarrow\psi(a_{j_{1}},\ldots,a_{j_{n}},\bar{b}),

and thus
\mathcal{M}\models\varphi(a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{n}})\leftrightarrow\varphi(a_{j_{1}},\ldots,a_{j_{n}})
.
∎

6.2.
Naming a structure

We continue working with a fixed
\mathcal{L}
-structure
\mathcal{M}
with universe
M
.

So far we have shown that if
Q\subseteq M
is enumerated by an eventually indiscernible sequence over some set
A\subseteq M
, then
Q^{\mathcal{M}_{A}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is interdefinable with
(Q,=)
. However, in order to apply our preservation theorems, we need
Q
to be
definable
in
\mathcal{M}
. As discussed above, our strategy is to make
Q
definable by expanding the language. So we are now back in the classical setting of preserving tameness when naming a new predicate. The following is a fundamental notion from this area of the literature (e.g.,
[
CZ01
]
).

Definition 6.6
.

Fix a subset
Q\subseteq M
. Consider the structure
(\mathcal{M},Q)
obtained by expanding
\mathcal{M}
by a new predicate interpreted as
Q
. A formula of
(\mathcal{M},Q)
is
bounded
if it is of the form

\square_{1}y_{1}\in Q\ldots\square_{n}y_{n}\in Q\,\psi(x,y),

where
\square_{i}\in\{\forall,\exists\}
and
\psi(x,y)
is a an
\mathcal{L}
-formula. We say
Q
is
bounded in
\mathcal{M}
if all formulas of
(\mathcal{M},Q)
are equivalent (modulo
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{M},Q)
) to bounded formulas.

The following observation is a straightforward exercise.

Remark 6.7
.

If
Q\subseteq M
is bounded in
M
, then
Q^{(\mathcal{M},Q)}_{\textnormal{ind}}
coincides with
Q^{\mathcal{M}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
.

We can now explain the relevance of weakly minimal structures.

Fact 6.8
.

Let
Q
be a subset of
M
.

(a)

[
CZ01
]
Suppose
Q
is bounded in
M
. Then for any
\lambda\geq|\mathcal{L}|+\aleph_{0}
,
(\mathcal{M},Q)
is
\lambda
-stable if and only if
\mathcal{M}
and
Q^{\mathcal{M}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
are
\lambda
-stable.

(b)

[
CL20
]
If
\mathcal{M}
is weakly minimal, then
Q
is bounded in
\mathcal{M}_{A}
for any
\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{M}
.

Altogether, if
\mathcal{M}
is weakly minimal then we can orchestrate a setting in which the above preservation theorems are applicable by naming an eventually indiscernible sequence. Moreover, since the induced structure on this sequence is interdefinable with a pure set, we are free to then enrich further by
any
other structure. This brings us to the next definition.

Definition 6.9
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be an arbitrary structure (in some language) whose universe has size at most
|M|
. Then an
expansion of
\mathcal{M}
naming
\mathcal{N}
is a structure of the form
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{Q})
, where
\mathcal{Q}
is an isomorphic copy
of
\mathcal{N}
and the universe of
\mathcal{Q}
is some subset
Q\subseteq M
.

In order to solidify the previous definition, we give an example of naming a structure. Let
\mathcal{N}=(V,E)
be a graph. Then an expansion of
\mathcal{M}
naming
\mathcal{N}
is a structure of the form
(\mathcal{M},Q,R)
where
Q
is a new (i.e., not in
\mathcal{L}
) unary predicate naming a subset of
M
and
R
is a new binary relation on
Q
such that
\mathcal{Q}\coloneqq(Q,R)
is an isomorphic copy of
\mathcal{N}=(V,E)
.

We can now prove the main result of this subsection.

Theorem 6.10
.

Assume
\mathcal{L}
is countable and
\mathcal{M}
is weakly minimal. Let
\mathcal{N}
be a stable (resp., superstable,
\mathrm{NIP}
,
\mathrm{NTP}_{2}
) countable structure. Then there is a stable (resp., superstable,
\mathrm{NIP}
,
\mathrm{NTP}_{2}
) expansion
\mathcal{M}^{*}
of
\mathcal{M}
naming
\mathcal{N}
. Moreover:

(i)

If
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
are
\omega
-stable, then so is
\mathcal{M}^{*}
.

(ii)

If
Q\subseteq M
is the universe of the copy of
\mathcal{N}
in
\mathcal{M}^{*}
, then
Q^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is interdefinable with
\mathcal{N}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}\preceq\mathcal{M}
be a countable elementary substructure. Without loss of generality, we can expand
\mathcal{L}
by constants for the universe
A
of
\mathcal{A}
. By Facts
6.2
and
6.3
we may fix a subset
Q\subseteq M
, which is enumerated by an eventually totally indiscernible sequence over
A
. Then
Q^{\mathcal{M}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is interdefinable with
(Q,=)
by Lemma
6.5
. In particular,
Q^{\mathcal{M}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is
\lambda
-stable for all
\lambda\geq\aleph_{0}
. Applying Fact
6.8
we have that
T\coloneqq\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{M},Q)
is superstable. Moreover, if
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{M})
is
\omega
-stable then so is
T
.

We now apply the material in Section
3
to the base theory
T
and
\emptyset
-definable set
Q
(which is stably embedded since
T
is stable). Note that the induced structure
Q^{T}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is still interdefinable with a pure set by Remark
6.7
and Fact
6.8
(b)
. Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a copy of
\mathcal{N}
expanding
Q^{T}_{\textnormal{ind}}
(without loss of generality, we may add any necessary constants to
\mathcal{N}
), and let
\mathcal{M}^{*}
be the corresponding expansion of
(\mathcal{M},Q)
by
\mathcal{Q}
. Then
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{M}^{*})
is
T[\mathcal{Q}]
. Altogether, the preservation of stability, superstability,
\omega
-stability, NIP, and NTP
2
now follows from Theorem
3.3
, Fact
3.6
, and Proposition
3.7
. Finally, note that
Q^{\mathcal{M}^{*}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is interdefinable with
\mathcal{N}
by Proposition
2.7
.
∎

Remark 6.11
.

Theorem
6.10
omits the dividing lines of simplicity and NSOP
1
because our preservation results for these properties require the additional hypothesis of algebraic embeddedness. So we point out that if
\mathcal{M}
is weakly minimal and contains an eventually indiscernible sequence that enumerates a set
Q
that is algebraically embedded in
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{M},Q)
then, using Corollary
4.36
(resp., Theorem
4.35
), an analogous argument works to build a simple (resp., NSOP
1
) expansion of
\mathcal{M}
naming some arbitrary simple (resp., NSOP
1
) countable structure
\mathcal{N}
. At the moment, we do not know whether every weakly minimal structure admits an eventually indiscernible sequence that is algebraically embedded after being named by a new predicate. However, in Section
7.2
we will construct such sequences in the special case of
(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
.

As a counterpoint to Remark
6.11
, we note that interpolative fusions
[
KTW22
]
provide a different method for adding simplicity and
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
in Theorem
6.10
.

Proposition 6.12
.

Assume
\mathcal{L}
is countable and
\mathcal{M}
is weakly minimal. Let
\mathcal{N}
be a simple (resp.,
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
) countable structure. Then there is a simple (resp.,
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
) expansion of
\mathcal{M}
naming
\mathcal{N}
.

Proof.

First assume
\mathcal{N}
is
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
. Let
T
and
\mathcal{Q}
be as in the proof of Theorem
6.10
. Then
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
by Corollary
5.2
(a)
. Now, if
\mathcal{N}
is actually simple, then it is also
\mathrm{NTP}_{2}
, hence
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is
\mathrm{NTP}_{2}
by Proposition
3.7
. So
T[\mathcal{Q}]
is
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
and
\mathrm{NTP}_{2}
, and thus is simple (see
[
She90
, Theorem III.7.11]
or
[
KR20
, Corollary 8.5]
).
∎

Next we state a quick corollary about distal structures. The notion of distality was introduced by Simon as a means to understand “purely unstable” NIP theories. Subsequent work of Chernikov and Starchenko
[
CS18
]
established a connection between distality and “tame combinatorics” via regularity lemmas and the Erdős-Hajnal property for definable sets. It has since been discovered that even the property of having a distal expansion (which encompasses a much wider class within NIP) leads to similar combinatorial tameness results. In particular, there is a growing body of work on
stable
structures admitting distal expansions. To our knowledge, there is no previously established example of a stable expansion of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
which is known to
not
admit a distal expansion. So we take the opportunity to construct one now.

Corollary 6.13
.

Assume
\mathcal{L}
is countable and
\mathcal{M}
is weakly minimal. Then there is a superstable expansion
\mathcal{M}^{*}
of
\mathcal{M}
that does not admit a distal expansion.

Proof.

By Theorem
6.10
there is a superstable expansion
\mathcal{M}^{*}
of
\mathcal{M}
naming
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
for some prime
p>0
. Then
\mathcal{M}^{*}
has no distal expansion by
[
CS18
, Corollary 6.3]
.
∎

We finish this subsection with some remarks on the assumption of weak minimality, which was primarily used to get the boundedness condition on subsets of
M
for free (via Fact
6.8
(b)
). An alternate route is via the notion of smallness.

Definition 6.14
.

Fix a subset
Q\subseteq M
. Then
Q
is
small in
\mathcal{M}
if there is some
(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},Q^{\prime})\equiv(\mathcal{M},Q)
such that, for any finite set
B\subseteq M^{\prime}
, any
\mathcal{L}
-type over
BQ^{\prime}
is realized in
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

In
[
CZ01
, Proposition 2.1]
, Casanovas and Ziegler show that if
\mathcal{M}
is nfcp and
Q
is small in
\mathcal{M}
, then
Q
is bounded in
\mathcal{M}
. Thus, instead of assuming
\mathcal{M}
is weakly minimal, one could instead assume
\mathcal{M}
is nfcp and attempt to a name a new structure on an eventually indiscernible sequence that enumerates a small set in
\mathcal{M}
. In general, such a sequence need not enumerate a small set, but in practice showing sets are small can often be easier than proving boundedness. For example, if
Q\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
is enumerated by an eventually indiscernible sequence in
(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
, then
Q
is automatically small in
(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
by Lemma
6.5
and
[
CL20
, Corollary 3.19
(a)
]
(which holds more generally for any
U
-rank
1
group with “finite torsion”).

Finally, we note that an NIP analogue of Fact
6.8
(a)
was proved by Chernikov and Simon
[
CS13
, Corollary 2.5]
. In particular, if
\mathcal{M}
is NIP and
Q\subseteq M
is bounded in
\mathcal{M}
then
(\mathcal{M},Q)
is NIP. Therefore, starting with the assumption that
\mathcal{M}
is NIP, if one can find a set
Q\subseteq M
that is bounded in
\mathcal{M}
, stably embedded in
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{M},Q)
,
and
enumerated by an eventually indiscernible sequence in
M
, then one can similarly use
Q
to build NIP (resp., NTP
2
) expansions of
\mathcal{M}
naming arbitrary NIP (resp., NTP
2
) countable structures.

6.3.
Expansion by mutually algebraic structure

The goal of this subsection is to prove the main result stated in Section
1.3
. We first recall the notion of a “mutually algebraic” relation on a set
A
(introduced by Laskowski in
[
Las13
]
).

Definition 6.15
.

Let
A
be a set and
R\subseteq A^{n}
an
n
-ary relation on
A
, for some
n\geq 1
. Then
R
is a
mutually algebraic relation on
A
if there is some
N\geq 1
such that for any
1\leq i\leq n
and any
b\in A
,

\lvert{\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})\in A^{n-1}:(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},b,a_{i},\ldots,a_{n-1})\in R\}}\rvert\leq N.

Example 6.16
.

Fix a set
A
.

(1)

Any unary relation
R\subseteq A
is mutually algebraic.

(2)

Suppose
R\subseteq A\times A
defines a graph relation on
A
. Then
R
is mutually algebraic if and only if the graph has bounded degree.

(3)

Suppose
R\subseteq A\times A
defines the graph of a function from
A
to
A
. Then
R
is mutually algebraic if and only if the function is
({\leq\!N})
-to-one for some
N\geq 1
.

Now let
\mathcal{A}
be an arbitrary
\mathcal{L}
-structure with universe
A
. Then an
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula is
mutually algebraic (in
\mathcal{A}
)
if it defines a mutually algebraic relation on
A
; and the structure
\mathcal{A}
is
mutually algebraic
if every
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formula is equivalent (in
\mathcal{A}
) to a Boolean combination of mutually algebraic
\mathcal{L}_{A}
-formulas.

Fact 6.17

(Laskowski
[
Las13
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an
\mathcal{L}
-structure.

(a)

\mathcal{A}
is mutually algebraic if and only if
\operatorname{Th}(\mathcal{A})
is superstable of
U
-rank
1
with trivial forking.

(b)

Assume
\mathcal{A}
is mutually algebraic, and let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be an expansion of
\mathcal{A}
by arbitrarily many mutually algebraic relations on
A
. Then
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is mutually algebraic.

Given a set
A
, let
A^{\textnormal{ma}}
denote the collection of all mutually algebraic relations on
A
.

Theorem 6.18
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a stable (resp., superstable) structure. Fix a definable set
A\subseteq M
such that
A^{\mathcal{M}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is mutually algebraic. Then
(\mathcal{M},A^{\textnormal{ma}})
is stable (resp., superstable).

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be the expansion of
A^{\mathcal{M}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
obtained by naming all sets in
A^{\textnormal{ma}}
. Then
\mathcal{A}
is mutually algebraic by Fact
6.17
(b)
, and thus superstable by Fact
6.17
(a)
. So
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{A})
is stable (resp., superstable) by Theorem
3.3
. Finally, note that
(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{A})
is interdefinable with
(\mathcal{M},A^{\textnormal{ma}})
.
∎

For weakly minimal structures we can use Fact
6.8
(b)
to remove the definability assumption from the previous theorem.

Corollary 6.19
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a weakly minimal structure with constants for an elementary substructure. Fix a subset
A\subseteq M
such that
A^{\mathcal{M}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is mutually algebraic. Then
(\mathcal{M},A^{\textnormal{ma}})
is superstable.

Proof.

Note that
A^{\mathcal{M}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is superstable by Fact
6.17
(a)
, and so
(\mathcal{M},A)
is superstable by Fact
6.8
. By Remark
6.7
,
A^{\mathcal{M}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
coincides with
A^{(\mathcal{M},A)}_{\textnormal{ind}}
. So we can apply Theorem
6.18
to
(\mathcal{M},A)
.
∎

7.
Tame expansions of
(\mathbb{Z},+)

7.1.
U
-rank in stable expansions of
(\mathbb{Z},+)

Recall that the present article was motivated in large part by Question
1
, which we restate here.

Question 7.1
.

(a)

Is there a strictly stable expansion of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
?

(b)

Is there a superstable expansion of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
with
U
-rank not equal to
\omega
?

Positive answers to both questions now follow immediately from the following special case of Theorem
6.10
.

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a stable (resp., superstable,
\mathrm{NIP}
,
\mathrm{NTP}_{2}
) countable structure. Then there is a stable (resp., superstable,
\mathrm{NIP}
,
\mathrm{NTP}_{2}
) expansion
\mathcal{Z}
of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
naming
\mathcal{N}
. Moreover, the
U
-rank of
\mathcal{Z}
is at least that of
\mathcal{N}
.

Proof.

Since
(\mathbb{Z},+)
is superstable of
U
-rank
1
, Theorem
6.10
yields the desired expansion
\mathcal{Z}
of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
, which names
\mathcal{N}
on some set
Q\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
. The lower bound on the
U
-rank of
\mathcal{Z}
comes from the fact that
Q^{\mathcal{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is interdefinable with
\mathcal{N}
.
∎

In particular, we can obtain a strictly stable expansion of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
by choosing
\mathcal{N}
to be any countable strictly stable structure (e.g., a nonabelian free group); and we can obtain a superstable expansion of
U
-rank greater than
\omega
by choosing
\mathcal{N}
to be any countable superstable structure of
U
-rank greater than
\omega
(e.g., an
(\omega+1)
-ordered chain of infinitely expanding equivalence relations with infinite classes).

A more meticulous version of Question
7.1
(b)
would ask for the precise identification of all ordinals
\alpha
for which there is a superstable expansion of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
with
U
-rank
\alpha
. So let us summarize what is known. As previously mentioned, a large number of examples with
U
-rank
\omega
can be found in previous work of several authors
[
Con19
,
Con18
,
CL20
,
Haw22
,
LP20
,
PS18
]
. Beyond this, Theorem
7.2
implies the existence of superstable expansions of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
of arbitrarily high countable
U
-rank, although the proof does not provide a way to construct one with a specifically chosen
U
-rank. In fact, certain
U
-ranks are impossible to obtain. The following is the current state of the art in this direction (to our knowledge).

Fact 7.3

(
[
BL86
,
PS18
]
)
.

Every proper superstable expansion of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
has infinite monomial
U
-rank.

Proof.

First, we recall the theorem of Palacín and Sklinos
[
PS18
]
that there is no proper stable expansion of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
with finite
U
-rank.
8
8

8

This was later strengthened to
dp
-rank in
[
CP18
]
.
Second, we recall a classical result of Berline and Lascar
[
BL86
, Corollary IV.2.7]
that if
G
is a superstable group of
U
-rank
\omega^{\alpha_{1}}k_{1}+\ldots+\omega^{\alpha_{n}}k_{n}
(in Cantor normal form) then, for any
1\leq r\leq n
,
G
has a definable normal subgroup of
U
-rank
\omega^{\alpha_{1}}k_{1}+\ldots+\omega^{\alpha_{r}}k_{r}
. Since any nontrivial subgroup of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
has finite index, it then follows from the Berline-Lascar inequalities for superstable groups
[
BL86
, Corollary III.8.2]
that the
U
-rank of any superstable expansion of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
must be a monomial
\omega^{\alpha}k
.
∎

7.2.
Vaporous sets of integers

Our next goal is to extend Theorem
7.2
to include the dividing lines of simplicity and NSOP
1
. Recall from Remark
6.11
that if
\mathcal{M}
is weakly minimal, and
\mathcal{N}
is an arbitrary simple (resp., NSOP
1
) structure, then the main obstacle in using the strategy of Theorem
6.10
to build a simple (resp., NSOP
1
) expansion of
\mathcal{M}
naming
\mathcal{N}
is whether one can find an eventually indiscernible sequence in
\mathcal{M}
that enumerates a set that is algebraically embedded after being named by a new predicate. We will show here that this is possible in the special case of
\mathcal{M}=(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
. Note that a direct application of Proposition
6.12
yields a simple (resp., NSOP
1
) expansion of
\mathcal{M}
naming
\mathcal{N}
, without the need to check for algebraic embeddedness.

Throughout this section we will use the fact that the theory of
(\mathbb{Z},+,0)
has quantifier elimination after adding adding binary relations for congruence mod
n
for all
n\geq 2
.

Definition 7.4
.

A strictly increasing sequence
(a_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}
from
\mathbb{Z}^{+}
is
vaporous
if the following two properties hold:

(i)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_{n+1}}{a_{n}}=\infty
.

(ii)

For all
m>0
,
(a_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}
is eventually constant modulo
m
.

The canonical example of a vaporous sequence is the factorials:
a_{n}=n!
. In
[
PS18
]
, it is shown that the
(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
-induced structure on the factorials is a pure infinite set. Therefore the factorials are eventually totally indiscernible over
\mathbb{Z}
by Lemma
6.5
. It turns out that the same holds for any vaporous sequence by various general results from
[
Con19
,
CL20
,
LP20
]
. Nevertheless, we will give a short self-contained proof (the details of which will be useful later).

Lemma 7.5
.

Suppose
(a_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}
is a strictly increasing sequence in
\mathbb{Z}^{+}
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_{n+1}}{a_{n}}=\infty
. Then for any
m,n>0
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}
, the equation

x_{1}+\ldots+x_{m}=y_{1}+\ldots+y_{n}+r

has only finitely many solutions in
\{a_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
satisfying
\max_{i}x_{i}\neq\max_{j}y_{j}
.

Proof.

Fix
m,n>0
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}
. Let
t=\max\{m,n\}
. By assumption, we can choose some
k\geq 0
such that if
i>k
then
a_{i+1}>ta_{i}+|r|
. Fix
i_{1},\ldots,i_{m},j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\geq 0
such that
a_{i_{1}}+\ldots+a_{i_{m}}=a_{j_{1}}+\ldots+a_{j_{n}}+r
. Let
i_{*}=\max\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}\}
and
j_{*}=\max\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\}
, and assume
i_{*}\neq j_{*}
. We show
i_{*},j_{*}\leq k
, which yields the lemma. Suppose instead that
\max\{i_{*},j_{*}\}>k
. If
i_{*}>j_{*}
then
i_{*}>k
, and thus

a_{j_{1}}+\ldots+a_{j_{n}}+r\leq ta_{j_{*}}+r<a_{i_{*}}\leq a_{i_{1}}+\ldots+a_{i_{m}}.

On the other hand, if
i_{*}<j_{*}
then
j_{*}>k
, and thus

a_{i_{1}}+\ldots+a_{i_{m}}-r\leq ta_{i_{*}}-r<a_{j_{*}}\leq a_{j_{1}}+\ldots+a_{j_{n}}.

So in either case, we have a contradiction.
∎

Corollary 7.6
.

Any vaporous sequence from
\mathbb{Z}^{+}
is eventually totally indiscernible over
\mathbb{Z}
with respect to
(\mathbb{Z},+)
.

Proof.

Fix a vaporous sequence
(a_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}
in
\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.
By quantifier elimination, it suffices to consider formulas of the form:

(1)

x_{1}+\ldots+x_{m}=y_{1}+\ldots+y_{n}+r
for some
m,n>0
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}
, or

(2)

x\equiv_{m}r
for some
0\leq r<m
.

If
\varphi(\bar{x},\bar{y})
is of the form in
(1)
then, by Lemma
7.5
, there is some
k
such that if
i_{1},\ldots,i_{m},j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}>k
are pairwise distinct then
\neg\varphi(a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{m}},a_{j_{1}},\ldots,a_{j_{n}})
holds (recall that
(a_{i})_{i=0}^{\infty}
is strictly increasing). On the other hand, if
\varphi(x)
is of the form in
(2)
, then by condition
(ii)
of Definition
7.4
there is some
k>0
such that either
\varphi(a_{i})
holds for all
i>k
or
\neg\varphi(a_{i})
holds for all
i>k
.
∎

We call an infinite subset
Q\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{+}

vaporous
if it can be enumerated by a (strictly increasing) vaporous sequence. The next lemma collects some model-theoretic facts about vaporous sets, which are all well-established in the literature.

Lemma 7.7
.

Suppose
Q\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{+}
is vaporous.

(a)

Q^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is interdefinable with
(Q,=)
.

(b)

Q
is small and bounded in
(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
.

Proof.

Part
(a)
follows from Corollary
7.6
and Lemma
6.5
. For part
(b)
, smallness follows from part
(a)
and
[
CL20
, Corollary 3.19
(a)
]
; and boundedness follows from Fact
6.8
(b)
.
9
9

9

Recall also that any small set in an nfcp structure is bounded by
[
CZ01
, Proposition 2.1]
. We also stress that Lemma
7.7
is largely evident from earlier work of Palacín and Sklinos
[
PS18
]
on the factorials, and also from various more general results in
[
Con19
]
and
[
LP20
]
.

∎

Remark 7.8
.

It follows from Lemma
7.7
that if
Q\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{+}
is vaporous then
(\mathbb{Z},+,Q)
is superstable. In fact, it is shown in
[
Con19
]
that the same conclusion holds for any set
Q\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{+}
enumerated by a sequence satisfying condition
(i)
of Definition
7.4
.

For the rest of this subsection, we let
Q
be a fixed vaporous subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. Define the theory
T=\operatorname{Th}(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1,Q)
in the language
\mathcal{L}=\{+,0,1,Q\}
. Let
\mathcal{U}\models T
be a monster model and let
\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }
denote forking independence in
T
.

Our next goal is to show that
Q
is algebraically embedded in
T
. Note first that Lemma
7.7
(a)
yields weak elimination of imaginaries for
\operatorname{Th}(Q^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}})
. By Remark
4.13
(b)
, this allows us to focus on the operator
\overline{A}^{r}=\mathrm{acl}_{T}(A)\cap Q(\mathcal{U})
on subsets
A\subseteq\mathcal{U}
. In order to prove that
Q
is algebraically embedded in
T
, it suffices to show:

(
\dagger
)

If
A,B,C\subseteq\mathcal{U}
and
\textstyle A\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{C}B
, then
\overline{ABC}^{r}\subseteq\mathrm{acl}_{T}(\overline{AC}^{r},\overline{BC}^{r})
.

We will start by giving a precise identification of the operator
\overline{A}^{r}
. The first step is the following consequence of Lemma
7.5
.

Corollary 7.9
.

Fix
a\in\mathcal{U}
and suppose that
ma=c_{1}v_{1}+\ldots+c_{n}v_{n}
for some
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, and pairwise distinct
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in Q(\mathcal{U})
. Then
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in\mathrm{acl}_{T}(a)
.

Proof.

We claim that the equation
ma=c_{1}x_{1}+\ldots+c_{n}x_{n}
has only finitely many solutions in
Q(\mathcal{U})^{n}
where the
x_{i}
’s are pairwise distinct. Note that if
a\in\mathbb{Z}
then this follows from Lemma
7.5
(and elementarity). To extend the result to
a\in\mathcal{U}
, it suffices to show that in the context of Lemma
7.5
, one can bound the number of solutions independently of the integer
r
. Given the statement of Lemma
7.5
, this improvement follows using a direct pigeonhole argument (which we leave to the reader). Alternatively, since
Q
is small in
(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
, and both
\operatorname{Th}(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
and
\operatorname{Th}(Q^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}})
are nfcp, one obtains nfcp for
T
by
[
CZ01
, Proposition 5.7]
. Thus
T
eliminates
\exists^{\infty}
by
[
She90
, Theorem II.4.4]
, which also yields the desired result.
∎

Let
T_{0}=\operatorname{Th}(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
in the language
\mathcal{L}_{0}=\{+,0,1\}
, and view (the
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-reduct of)
\mathcal{U}
as a monster model of
T_{0}
. By quantifier elimination, if
A\subseteq\mathcal{U}
then
\mathrm{acl}_{T_{0}}(A)
is the (relative) divisible hull of the subgroup generated by
A
.
The previous corollary motivates the following definition.

Definition 7.10
.

Given
A\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, the
Q
-projection of
A
, denoted
Q\textnormal{Pr}(A)
, is the set of all
v\in Q(\mathcal{U})
for which there exist pairwise distinct
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in Q(\mathcal{U})
and
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
such that
v=v_{i}
for some
i
and
\sum c_{i}v_{i}\in\mathrm{acl}_{T_{0}}(A)
.

Remark 7.11
.

Fix
A\subseteq\mathcal{U}
.

(a)

Since
\mathbb{Z}\subseteq\mathrm{acl}_{T_{0}}(A)
, it follows by definition that
Q(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq Q\textnormal{Pr}(A)
.

(b)

Given
v\in Q(\mathcal{U})
, we have
v\in Q\textnormal{Pr}(A)
if and only if
v\in\mathrm{acl}_{T_{0}}(A\cup(Q(\mathcal{U})\backslash\{v\}))
.

Recall that for any
A\subseteq\mathcal{U}
,
\overline{A}^{r}
denotes
\mathrm{acl}(A)\cap Q
.

Proposition 7.12
.

If
A\subseteq\mathcal{U}
then
\overline{A}^{r}=Q\textnormal{Pr}(A)
.

Proof.

Fix
A\subseteq\mathcal{U}
. Note that
Q\textnormal{Pr}(A)
is contained in
Q(\mathcal{U})
by definition, and contained in
\mathrm{acl}_{T}(A)
by Corollary
7.9
. So it suffices to show
\overline{A}^{r}\subseteq Q\textnormal{Pr}(A)
. Without loss of generality, we may assume
|A|
is small enough to ensure
|Q(\mathcal{U})|>|Q\textnormal{Pr}(A)|
(recall
Q\textnormal{Pr}(A)\subseteq\mathrm{acl}_{T}(A)
). Set
V=Q(\mathcal{U})\backslash Q\textnormal{Pr}(A)
. We want to show
V\cap\mathrm{acl}_{T}(A)=\emptyset
.
Since
V
is infinite, it suffices to fix
u,v\in V
and show that
u\equiv^{T}_{A}v
.

Set
B=A\cup(Q(\mathcal{U})\backslash\{u,v\})
. We claim that
uv\equiv^{T_{0}}_{B}vu
. By quantifier elimination, and the fact that
\{u,v\}
is
\mathrm{acl}_{T_{0}}
-independent over
B
(by Remark
7.11
(b)
), we only need to check that
u
and
v
have the same remainder modulo
n
for all integers
n\geq 1
. Since
u,v\in Q(\mathcal{U})\backslash\mathbb{Z}
(by Remark
7.11
(a)
), this follows from the assumption that
Q
is vaporous (in particular, condition
(ii)
of Definition
7.4
).

We now have a partial
\mathcal{L}_{0}
-elementary map
f
, which fixes
B
pointwise and exchanges
u
and
v
. Since
Q
is bounded in
(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
, it follows that
f
is
\mathcal{L}
-elementary (see
[
CZ01
, Lemma 3.2]
). In particular, this shows
u\equiv^{T}_{A}v
.
∎

Theorem 7.13
.

Q
is algebraically embedded in
T=\operatorname{Th}(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1,Q)
.

Proof.

We will show that for any
\mathrm{acl}_{T_{0}}
-closed sets
A,B,C\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, with
C\subseteq A\cap B
, if
A\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{C}B
then
Q\textnormal{Pr}(AB)\subseteq Q\textnormal{Pr}(A)\cup Q\textnormal{Pr}(B)
. Note that this implies the statement
(\dagger)
above by Proposition
7.12
and basic properties of forking independence (mainly Remark
4.2
(i)
). So fix
\mathrm{acl}_{T_{0}}
-closed
A,B,C\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, with
C\subseteq A\cap B
, and assume
A\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}^{T}_{C}B
. Fix some
v_{0}\in Q\textnormal{Pr}(AB)
. Then there are
a\in A
,
b\in B
, pairwise distinct
v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\in Q(\mathcal{U})
, which are distinct from
v_{0}
, and
c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
, such that

c_{0}v_{0}+\ldots+c_{n}v_{n}=a+b.

Since
T
is superstable, there is a finite subset
D\subseteq C
such that
a\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle T$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }_{D}b
. Suppose first that
b\in\mathrm{acl}_{T_{0}}(Q(\mathcal{U})D)
. Then we have an identity of the form

(
\dagger
)

mb=d_{1}w_{1}+\ldots+d_{k}w_{k}+c

for some pairwise distinct
w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}\in Q(\mathcal{U})
,
c\in D
,
d_{1},\ldots,d_{k},m\in\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{0\}
. So

(
\dagger\dagger
)

mc_{0}v_{0}+\ldots+mc_{n}v_{n}=ma+d_{1}w_{1}+\ldots+d_{k}w_{k}+c

If
v_{0}=w_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq k
, then
(\dagger)
implies that
v_{0}\in Q\textnormal{Pr}(mb-c)\subseteq Q\textnormal{Pr}(B)
. So we may assume
v_{0}\neq w_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
. Then, after some rearranging and renaming,
(\dagger\dagger)
gives us an identity of the form

mc_{0}v_{0}+e_{1}u_{1}+\ldots+e_{\ell}u_{\ell}=ma+c

where
u_{1},\ldots,u_{\ell}\in Q(\mathcal{U})
are pairwise distinct, and distinct from
v_{0}
. So
v_{0}\in Q\textnormal{Pr}(ma+c)\subseteq Q\textnormal{Pr}(A)
.

Finally, suppose
b\not\in\mathrm{acl}_{T_{0}}(Q(\mathcal{U})D)
. Then, since
Q
is small in
(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
and D is finite, we can construct a sequence
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
in
\mathcal{U}
such that for all
i<\omega
,
b_{i}\equiv^{T_{0}}_{Q(\mathcal{U})D}b
and
b_{i}\not\in\mathrm{acl}_{T_{0}}(Q(\mathcal{U})Db_{<i})
. Since
Q
is bounded in
(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
, it then follows from
[
CZ01
, Lemma 3.2]
that
b_{i}\equiv^{T}_{D}b
for all
i<\omega
.

Now define the
\mathcal{L}
-formula

\varphi(x,y):=\exists v_{0}\in Q\ldots\exists v_{n}\in Q(c_{0}v_{0}+\ldots+c_{n}v_{n}=x+y).

Note that
\varphi(a,b)
holds.
We show that
\{\varphi(x,b_{i}):i<\omega\}
is
2
-inconsistent, which contradicts
a\mathop{\mathchoice{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\displaystyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\textstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptstyle{}}{\kern 5.71527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\mid$\hss}\lower 3.87495pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\kern 5.71527pt\scriptscriptstyle{}}}^{T}_{D}b
. So fix
i<j<\omega
and suppose we have
a^{*}\models\varphi(x,b_{i})\wedge\varphi(x,b_{j})
. Then there are
v^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,v^{\prime}_{n},v^{\prime\prime}_{1},\ldots,v^{\prime\prime}_{n}\in Q(\mathcal{U})
such that
a^{*}+b_{i}=c_{0}v^{\prime}_{0}+\ldots+c_{n}v^{\prime}_{n}
and
a^{*}+b_{j}=c_{0}v^{\prime\prime}_{0}+\ldots+c_{n}v^{\prime\prime}_{n}
. Then we have

b_{j}=c_{0}v^{\prime\prime}_{0}+\ldots+c_{n}v^{\prime\prime}_{n}-a^{*}=c_{0}v^{\prime\prime}_{0}+\ldots+c_{n}v^{\prime\prime}_{n}-(c_{0}v^{\prime}_{0}+\ldots+c_{n}v^{\prime}_{n})+b_{i},

Thus
b_{j}\in\mathrm{acl}_{T_{0}}(Q(\mathcal{U})b_{i})
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Remark 7.14
.

Theorem
7.13
can be generalized to any set
Q\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{+}
enumerated by a sequence satisfying just condition
(i)
of Definition
7.4
. More generally, one only needs the conclusion of Lemma
7.5
. In this case,
Q^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is interdefinable with an expansion of
(Q,=)
by various unary predicates (namely, those for
Q\cap(m\mathbb{Z}+r)
for all integers
m,r
). Therefore
Q
is still small (and thus also bounded) in
(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
by, e.g.,
[
CL20
, Corollary 3.19
(a)
]
. We also note that
Q^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
still has weak elimination of imaginaries since this is true for any theory involving only unary relations
10
10

10

It is easy to check that in such a theory, the
11
axiom holds for algebraic independence
\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle a$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }
over any algebraically closed set, and thus
\mathop{\ \ \hbox to0.0pt{\hss$|^{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle a$\hss}}$\hss}\lower 4.30554pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\smile$\hss}\ \ }
satisfies the criterion for weak elimination of imaginaries in
[
MRK21
, Proposition 1.17]
.
. The only other use of condition
(ii)
of Definition
7.4
was in the proof of Proposition
7.12
when analyzing the set
V
. But one could instead partition
V
using cosets of the divisible subgroup of
\mathcal{U}
, and run the same analysis on each piece. By compactness, each piece in this partition of
V
is either empty or of unbounded cardinality, and so the argument goes through.

We can now extend Theorem
7.2
to also include simplicity and NSOP
1
.

Corollary 7.15
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a simple (resp.,
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
) countable structure. Then there is a simple (resp.,
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
) expansion of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
naming
\mathcal{N}
.

Proof.

Let
Q\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{+}
be vaporous, and set
T=\operatorname{Th}(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1,Q)
. Then
T
is stable, and
Q
is algebraically embedded in
T
by Theorem
7.13
. So we may apply Corollary
4.36
(resp., Theorem
4.35
) where
\mathcal{Q}
is a copy of
\mathcal{N}
with universe
Q
.
∎

7.3.
Expansions by unary sets

The goal of this subsection is to prove the results summarized in Sections
1.1
and
1.2
. We first use vaporous sets to show that any countable graph can be coded into an expansion of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
by some unary predicate, while preserving various levels of model-theoretic complexity.

Theorem 7.16
.

Suppose
Q\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{+}
is vaporous. Let
E
be a graph relation on
Q
, and set
A=Q\cup\{a+b:(a,b)\in E\}
.

(a)

(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
is interdefinable with
(\mathbb{Z},+,Q,E)
.

(b)

(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
is stable (resp., superstable, simple,
\mathrm{NIP}
,
\mathrm{NTP}_{2}
,
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
) if and only if
(Q,E)
is stable (resp., superstable, simple,
\mathrm{NIP}
,
\mathrm{NTP}_{2}
,
\mathrm{NSOP}_{1}
). Moreover, the
U
-rank of
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
is at least that of
(Q,E)
.

Proof.

Part
(a)
. Obviously
A
is definable in
(\mathbb{Z},+,Q,E)
. For the other direction, we first show that
Q
is definable in
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
. Consider a formula
\varphi(x)
, which says that
x\in A
and
x
is the sum of two distinct elements in
A
. Clearly
A\backslash Q\subseteq\varphi(\mathbb{Z})
. Moreover, Lemma
7.5
(with
m=1
,
n\in\{2,3,4\}
, and
r=0
) implies that only finitely many elements of
Q
satisfy
\varphi(x)
. So
A(x)\wedge\neg\varphi(x)
defines some cofinite subset of
Q
, which suffices to prove that
Q
is definable in
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
.

Next we show
E
is definable in
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
. Consider a formula
\psi(x,y)
, which says that
x,y\in Q
and
x+y\in A
. Clearly
E\subseteq\psi(\mathbb{Z})
. Moreover, Lemma
7.5
(with
m=2
,
n\in\{1,2\}
, and
r=0
) implies that
\psi(\mathbb{Z})\backslash E
is finite.

Part
(b)
. By Corollary
7.6
and Lemma
6.5
,
\operatorname{Th}(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1,Q,E)
is precisely
T[\mathcal{Q}]
where
T=\operatorname{Th}(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1,Q)
and
\mathcal{Q}
is the expansion of
(Q,E)
by constants for all elements of
Q
. Recall also that
Q
is algebraically embedded in
T
by Theorem
7.13
. Altogether, the first claim follows from part
(a)
and the preservation theorems above. For the second claim, recall that the
(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1,Q,E)
-induced structure on
Q
is interdefinable with
(Q,E)
by Proposition
2.7
. It follows that the
U
-rank of
(\mathbb{Z},+,Q,E)
is at least that of
(Q,E)
, and so the same is true of
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
.
∎

We can now give another positive answer to Question
1
(restated above in Question
7.1
) using expansions of
(\mathbb{Z},+)
by unary predicates.

Definition 7.17
.

Let
\mathsf{UG}
be the set of all ordinals
\alpha
such that there is a superstable (pure) graph of
U
-rank at least
\alpha
.

Corollary 7.18
.

There is a set
A_{\infty}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
(\mathbb{Z},+,A_{\infty})
is strictly stable. Moreover, for any
\alpha\in\mathsf{UG}
there is a set
A_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
(\mathbb{Z},+,A_{\alpha})
is superstable of
U
-rank at least
\alpha
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Theorem
7.16
, together with the definition of
\mathsf{UG}
and the existence of strictly stable graphs (see
[
HMS83
]
for an explicit description of such a graph).
∎

Remark 7.19
.

It is a well-known fact that every first-order structure in a finite language is bi-interpretable with a graph (see
[
Hod93
, Theorem 5.5.1]
). Consequently, if there is a superstable theory
T
in a finite language with
U(T)\geq\alpha\omega
, then
\alpha\in\mathsf{UG}
by sub-additivity of
U
-rank. Thus we conjecture that
\mathsf{UG}
is the set of all countable ordinals, since there ought to be theories in finite languages with arbitrarily high countable
U
-ranks. However, we have so far been unable to find a reference or a proof of this. In light of existing literature, it appears the most we can say is that
\mathsf{UG}
contains
\omega^{m}
for all
m>0
(e.g., witnessed by DCF
0,m

[
McG00
]
). Beyond this, a possible lead is unpublished work of Bouscaren and Ziegler
[
BZ92
]
, in which the authors describe a particular interpretation of an
\mathcal{L}
-structure in a graph, with
\mathcal{L}
countable (and possibly infinite). Using this construction, they show that Vaught’s Conjecture reduces to theories of graphs. In personal communication, Ziegler suggested that their interpretation should preserve superstability.

Next, we give a concrete formulation of the result alluded to at the start of Section
1.3
,
namely, the existence of sets
B\subseteq A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
is stable and
(\mathbb{Z},+,B)
is unstable. Indeed, by Remark
7.8
and Theorem
7.16
(a)
, we have the following general observation.

Proposition 7.20
.

Let
Q\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{+}
be a vaporous set, and define
A=Q\cup(Q+Q)
. Then
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
is superstable, but for any countable graph
\Gamma
there is some
B\subseteq A
such that
\Gamma
is definable in
(\mathbb{Z},+,B)
.

Finally, we use similar techniques to construct sets
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that the induced structure
A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
has large
U
-rank. As discussed in Section
1.1
, all examples in previous literature of stable expansions
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
are such that
U(A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}})=1
.

Corollary 7.21
.

(a)

There is a set
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is strictly stable.

(b)

For any
n<\omega
, there is a set
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
U(A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}})=n
.

(c)

Suppose
\alpha
is an ordinal such that
\mathsf{UG}
contains some
\beta>\alpha\omega
. Then there is a set
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
U(A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}})>\alpha
.

Proof.

Part
(a)
. Let
A=A_{\infty}
be as in Corollary
7.18
. Then
(\mathbb{Z},+,A_{\infty})
is strictly stable, and thus so is
A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
by Fact
6.8
.

Part
(b)
. Fix
n<\omega
. We may assume
n\geq 1
. Let
Q=\{k!:k\geq n\}
. It is easy to check that it follows by the definition of
Q
that the map
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\mapsto x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}
from
Q^{n}
to
\mathbb{N}
is injective. Note that
U(Q^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}})=1
since
Q^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is interdefinable with
(Q,=)
. Define

B=\{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}:\bar{x}\in Q^{n},~x_{i}>n!\text{ for some $1<i\leq n$}\}.

Let
A=B\cup Q
. Then it follows from Lemma
7.5
(similar to the proof of Theorem
7.16
(a)
) that
B\cap Q
is finite and
Q
is an
A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
-definable subset of
A
. So
U(A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}})\geq n
since we have an injective definable map from
(Q\backslash\{n!\})^{n}
into
A
. Conversely, we can definably interpret
A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
in
Q^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
with universe
Q^{n}
(send
Q\backslash B
into
Q\times\{n!\}^{n-1}
, and
B
to the complement). Therefore
U(A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}})\leq n
by Lascar’s inequality.

Part
(c)
. Given such an
\alpha
, let
\Gamma
be a graph of
U
-rank
\beta>\alpha\omega
. By Theorem
7.16
, there is a set
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
is superstable and defines
\Gamma
(using a unary set for the universe). Thus
(\mathbb{Z},+,A)
has
U
-rank at least
\beta
. So
A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}}
is superstable, and it follows from
[
Con19
, Theorem 2.11]
that
U(A^{\mathbb{Z}}_{\textnormal{ind}})>\alpha
.
∎

7.4.
Addendum on vaporous sequences

In the course of developing various results of eventually indiscernible sequences and then specializing to vaporous sequences in
\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, the authors wondered whether
any
strictly increasing sequence in
\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, which is eventually indiscernible in the structure
(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
, must be vaporous. So we have included some brief details showing that this is not the case. Note first that any eventually indiscernible sequence in
(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
must satisfy condition
(ii)
of Definition
7.4
; but we will see that condition
(i)
can fail.

Let
(p_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}
be a strictly increasing enumeration of all prime powers. The following is a special case of the (generalized) Chinese Remainder Theorem.

Fact 7.22
.

Fix
k\geq 1
, and suppose
b\in\mathbb{Z}
is such that
b\equiv_{p_{t}}0
for all
1\leq t<k
. Then there is some
0\leq r<\operatorname{lcm}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})
such that
r\equiv_{p_{k}}b
and
r\equiv_{p_{t}}0
for all
1\leq t<k
.

Lemma 7.23
.

Let
(b_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}
be any sequence of integers. Then there is a sequence
(a_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}
in
\mathbb{Z}
such that
|a_{n}-b_{n}|\leq n
for all
n\geq 0
and, for all
m>0
,
(a_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}
is eventually 0 modulo
m
.

Proof.

We first fix
n\geq 0
, and inductively define a sequence
(b_{n,k})_{k=0}^{\infty}
such that:

(i)

b_{n,k}\equiv_{p_{t}}0
for all
1\leq t\leq k
, and

(ii)

b_{n,k+1}=b_{n,k}+r_{n,k+1}
for some
0\leq r_{n,k+1}<\operatorname{lcm}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k+1})
.

Let
b_{n,0}=b_{n}
. Suppose we have
b_{n,0},\ldots,b_{n,k}
satisfying
(i)
and
(ii)
. Using
(i)
and Fact
7.22
, choose
0\leq r_{n,k+1}<\operatorname{lcm}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k+1})
such that
r_{n,k+1}\equiv_{p_{k+1}}\text{-}b_{n,k}
and
r_{n,k+1}\equiv_{p_{t}}0
for all
1\leq t\leq k
. Set
b_{n,k+1}=b_{n,k}+r_{n,k+1}
. To verify
(i)
, note that if
1\leq t\leq k
then
r_{n,k+1}\equiv_{p_{t}}0
by construction and
b_{n,k}\equiv_{p_{t}}0
by induction, and so
b_{n,k+1}\equiv_{p_{t}}0
. Moreover,
r_{n,k}\equiv_{p_{k+1}}\text{-}b_{n,k}
by construction, and so
b_{n,k+1}\equiv_{p_{k+1}}0
.

Set
N_{0}=0
and, for
k\geq 1
, set
N_{k}=\sum_{t=1}^{k}(\operatorname{lcm}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{t})-1)
. For any
n\geq 0
and
k\geq 0
, we have

b_{n,k}=b_{n}+r_{n,1}+\ldots+r_{n,k}\leq b_{n}+N_{k}.

Now for each
n\geq 0
, let
k_{n}\geq 0
be the maximal
k
such that
n\geq N_{k}
. Define
a_{n}=b_{n,k_{n}}
. Then
|a_{n}-b_{n}|\leq N_{k_{n}}\leq n
.

Finally, we show that
(a_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}
is eventually
0
modulo
m
for all
m>0
. It suffices to assume
m=p_{k}
for some
k\geq 1
. So fix
k\geq 1
. Suppose
n\geq N_{k}
. Then
k\leq k_{n}
, and so
a_{n}=b_{n,k_{n}}\equiv_{p_{k}}0
.
∎

Remark 7.24
.

After minor modifications to the proof, one can adjust the statement of the lemma so that
|a_{n}-b_{n}|\leq n
is replaced by
|a_{n}-b_{n}|\leq f(n)
, where
f(n)
is any divergent function.

Corollary 7.25
.

There is a sequence
(a_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}
in
\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, which is eventually indiscernible over
\mathbb{Z}
but not vaporous.

Proof.

Let
(a_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}
be as in Lemma
7.23
with respect to the starting sequence
b_{n}=\lfloor\pi^{n}\rfloor
. Set
Q=\{a_{n}:n\geq 0\}
. We claim that the induced structure on
Q
from
(\mathbb{Z},+,0,1)
is
(Q,=)
, and so
(a_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}
is eventually indiscernible over
\mathbb{Z}
by Lemma
6.5
. For formulas of the form
x\equiv_{m}r
this follows by construction. For the induced structure from linear equations, this is a special case of a more general family of examples considered in
[
Con18
]
(in particular, “independently sparse” sequences; see
[
Con18
, Remark 4.19]
).

To see that
(a_{n})_{n=0}^{\infty}
is not vaporous, note that
a_{n}=\pi^{n}+r_{n}
where
r_{n}
is real number with
|r_{n}|<n+1
. Therefore

\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_{n+1}}{a_{n}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\pi^{n+1}+r_{n+1}}{\pi^{n}+r_{n}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\pi+\frac{r_{n+1}}{\pi^{n}}}{1+\frac{r_{n}}{\pi^{n}}}=\pi.\qed
